CHARACTER: Brannoc Ashdown, Human cleric 3 (Life), acolyte background (as the sheet is now, after the last round; the level and HP may have been lower earlier)
== OPENING ==
Your character is complete.

Brannoc Ashdown is a young cleric of the Life domain from Millbrook Landing, a river town of fishermen and reed-cutters. The Grey Fever emptied the houses there when he was six, and the old temple healer, Mother Edda Thorne, raised him. She died last winter and left him her plain wooden amulet, and he left town to carry her work to places that have no healer. He is only a few weeks into that road.

Dusk is settling over a muddy back road in a country of thin farms and low hills. A small settlement huddles ahead, a scatter of crooked houses with its lanterns lit early and its doors barred. A dog barks once and stops. The air smells of wet earth, woodsmoke, and, faintly under it, blood.

A boy of about ten, mud to his knees, comes running out from between two fences and nearly slips as he catches your sleeve. "Please, are you a healer? That's a healer's amulet, isn't it? Please, sir, they're in the Harrow barn. My da, and Widow Harrow's boys, and... there's so many. Something came out of the woods last night. We've no one to help them. Please."

Behind him, past the last house, a big barn stands with its doors shut and a thin line of lamplight leaking from the cracks. Somewhere inside, someone is moaning. At the treeline beyond, nothing moves.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 1 ==
PLAYER: I kneel to the boy's level and put a hand on his shoulder. "I'm Brannoc, and I'm a healer. What's your name? Take me to your da, and I'll do everything I can." I keep my shield up and my mace ready as we hurry toward the barn, and I hum a low hymn to steady him.

DM: The boy swallows and nods. "Tam. I'm Tam. Da's Joss Fenner." Your hymn seems to help; his breathing slows, and he leads you at a half-run along the fence line, glancing at the dark trees every few steps.

At the barn door a gaunt woman in a stained apron stands with a hay fork, and two men flank her with a woodaxe and a hunting bow. They lower their weapons when they see the amulet and your mace. "A healer," the woman breathes. "Gods be thanked. I'm Widow Harrow. Quick, inside, and bar it behind you."

Lamplight, straw, and the smell of blood and fear. Maybe fifteen people crowd the barn, mostly women and children. Along the back wall, on straw pallets, lie five wounded. A big man, Tam's father, has a torn, bound thigh and a gray face. A boy of fourteen or so has a ragged bite across his shoulder, and the flesh around it looks angry and swollen. An older man gasps shallowly with a wrapped chest. Two others are quieter, and you can't tell yet whether that is better or worse.

"Wolves, we thought," says Widow Harrow low. "But wolves don't take a man's lantern, or stand upright at the fence and watch. It came again before dawn. We've been counting the hours until dark."

Tam tugs your hand toward his father. Joss Fenner's eyes find yours. "Boy's all right?" he rasps. "Don't mind me, see to the others."

You have two spell slots, your healing prayers, and a barn full of people who will be sitting in the dark again in an hour. What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 2 ==
PLAYER: I kneel by Joss first and take his hand. "Hush now, Tam's safe, and I'm not leaving anyone to suffer alone." I check the gray-faced man's bound thigh and the angry bite on the boy's shoulder, then ask Widow Harrow, "How long since each was hurt, and has anyone cleaned that bite? Fetch me clean water, boiled if you can, and linens."

While she does, I use Medicine to see who is worst off, and ask the two quiet ones' names so I can reach them before the dark comes.

DM: Your hands know this work. Joss's grip is strong but clammy, and you feel his pulse racing under your fingers as you ease the binding up.

Medicine (17): you read the room quickly.

Joss has lost a lot of blood, but the bleeding has slowed, and the binding was tied well. He is weak but steady, and he won't die in the next hour. The boy with the bitten shoulder is the one to watch. His name is Wil Harrow, and the wound was dressed with only a rag. It was never cleaned, and the swelling and heat are spreading. The old man with the wrapped chest is Old Garrit, and he is struggling for breath. The ribs under your fingers move wrongly, and he needs you soonest of anyone. The two quiet ones are brothers, Pell and Dorrin Harrow, Wil's elder brothers. Pell is asleep with a deep gash on his arm and is simply exhausted, but Dorrin lies still with his eyes half open, barely waking when you say his name, and his skin is cold and damp. He is bleeding inside, you think, or has taken a blow to the head.

Widow Harrow answers as she fetches water. "Joss and the old man were struck near dusk last night, and Wil at the fence just before dawn. Dorrin was the lantern-carrier when it came back this morning. Nobody's cleaned a thing. We boiled nothing, we were afraid to light the hearth." She hesitates. "It made no sound. Not a growl. That's what I keep thinking about."

Outside, the light is going. Maybe half an hour of usable dusk remains. You have two castings of healing prayer, and four people who could use them. Who do you tend first, and what do you do about the dark coming?

== ROUND 3 ==
PLAYER: I go to Old Garrit first, since his breathing is the most urgent, and cast Cure Wounds on him at 1st level with a hand on his chest, humming Edda's hymn softly. Then I'll turn to Dorrin and use my second slot on him, because a cold, barely waking man can't wait for me to clean wounds.

"Widow Harrow, light the hearth and boil that water. A fire in the dark is better than no light at all, and I'd rather see what's coming. Can your two men watch the barn door and the loft, and keep the bow ready?"

DM: You kneel by Old Garrit, lay your hand flat on his wrapped chest, and hum Edda's hymn. Warmth gathers under your palm, and you feel the wrongness in his ribs ease. His next breath goes deep, all the way down, and his eyes open wide. "Gods," he whispers. "Haven't breathed like that since yesterday." Color returns to his lips.

You turn to Dorrin and cup his cold face. The prayer comes again, and this time it takes longer, as though the warmth has to search for him. Then his eyelids flutter, his fingers close on your sleeve, and he mutters, "Ma?" His skin is no longer clammy, and his breathing is steady. He is awake, though weak. Your spell slots are spent now.

Widow Harrow nods once, tight-lipped. "Hearth, then." She sends a girl for kindling. The two men take the door and the loft ladder, the bowman crouched where he can see through a knothole. The hearth catches, small and bright, and the first kettle goes on. The barn smells less of fear and more of smoke and wet wool.

You still have Wil's bite, Joss's thigh, and Pell's gash, and only your hands, clean water, and the Medicine you've learned. Outside, true dark has fallen. The bowman at the knothole goes very still and raises one hand without turning around.

"Healer," he breathes. "Somebody's at the fence. Stood up straight, like a man. Hasn't moved."

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 4 ==
PLAYER: I quietly pick up my mace and shield and move to the knothole beside the bowman, keeping low and away from the lamplight. "Don't loose yet," I whisper. "Let me look. Widow Harrow, douse the lanterns except the hearth, and keep the children back from the door and away from the walls."

DM: You ease down beside the bowman, shield tucked in, and put your eye to the knothole. Behind you, Widow Harrow's hushed orders move through the barn. Lamps go out one by one, until only the low red hearth glows, and the children are hustled to the middle of the floor, away from the walls.

Perception (16): your eyes adjust to the dark. A thin moon is up behind the cloud, and it is enough.

About forty paces off, at the paddock fence, a figure stands motionless. It is tall and unnaturally thin, wrapped in rags that hang like wet bandages, with arms too long for the body, and it is a little stooped, as though listening. It holds something low at its side that catches the hearthlight as a faint glint: a farmer's lantern, unlit. Its face is turned toward the barn, pale and still. You never see it breathe. It does not shift its weight. A mist of cold breath should be rising in this chill air, and there is none.

Then you notice what else is wrong. The dog that barked once at the village edge has not made a sound since. Nothing at all is moving in the yard, as if the night itself were holding still around the thing.

The bowman's breath is shaking. "It's been standing there since dusk both nights," he whispers. "Then it comes for the door. Always the door."

You have your mace, your shield, a light crossbow, and no spells left. Behind you are fifteen people, five of them hurt. What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 5 ==
PLAYER: I stay low and keep my voice barely a breath. "It watches and waits for the door, so let's not give it one. Bar the door with the heaviest thing you've got, and have someone pile straw and hay by the entrance so we can light it if it breaks through; fire's better than nothing against a thing that doesn't breathe."

"I'll stand at the door with my shield. Keep your arrow on it, but only loose if it moves toward us. And Widow Harrow, does anyone here know of an old grave, a bad death, or a cursed place in those woods that this might have come from?"

DM: The barn moves quietly and quickly. Two men drag a grain bin against the double doors, and a third wedges a plough beam across the bar. The girl who fetched kindling and a pair of boys bring armfuls of loose straw, which you pile in a loose heap just inside the door, well clear of the hearth and the children. Widow Harrow checks the lamp oil with a hard eye and sets a covered lantern within reach of the pile.

You take your place at the door, shield up, mace in hand, and the bowman settles at the knothole. Outside, the figure has not moved.

Widow Harrow comes to your shoulder, wiping her hands on her apron. She answers in a murmur, not taking her eyes off the door. "A grave? No. But there's the Lamp. Wayfarers' Lamp, up the north track, a stone lamp-house in the beechwoods where old Hob Marrow kept a light burning for travellers. Never missed a night in forty years. He didn't come down for the harvest feast this year. Somebody went up and the lamp was cold, the door open, and no sign of him. We told ourselves he'd gone to his sister's in the valley. Then the lanterns started going missing from porches. Then the first night."

She swallows. "It always takes the lantern. Every light it can reach. And when it's got one, it stands and looks at the one that's still burning."

Behind you, in the dim, Tam is whispering to his father. Wil has begun to shiver, and Pell is stirring. At the knothole, the bowman hisses: "It's lifted its head. It's looking at the hearth smoke."

Through the cracks of the door you can just see the pale shape begin to walk, slow and even, toward the barn.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 6 ==
PLAYER: I set my feet behind the barred door, shield braced against it and mace ready, and speak low over my shoulder. "Widow Harrow, hold the lantern ready by the straw, but don't light it until I say. Bowman, wait until it's close enough that you can't miss, and aim for the head or chest."

Then I whisper, half to myself, "Hob Marrow kept that light forty years for strangers. Whatever this is, it's wearing his lantern, and I want to know why." I keep my voice steady so the children hear calm, and I hum Edda's hymn under my breath.

DM: You plant your boots and your shoulder against the bar. Wood presses cool against your shield arm. Behind you, Widow Harrow's hands steady on the lantern, and the bowman draws a slow breath and holds it. Your hymn is barely a thread, and one of the children stops sniffling to listen.

The footsteps are slow, even, and wet, the sound of mud being walked through without hurry. They cross the yard and stop. Through the seam between the doors you see the dull shine of a pale hand lifted, and a lantern held up toward the crack as if to show it. Rain-darkened rags hang from its wrists. A thin smell comes through the gap, old lamp oil, damp earth, and under it something sweetish and cold.

It does not strike the door. It does not push. It simply stands there, holding the lantern up, unlit, with its head tilted as though waiting to be let in.

Then, so quietly that you can barely tell it from the wind, a dry voice scrapes through the wood: "...light."

The bowman's arrow trembles. Across the barn, Joss Fenner draws a sharp breath, and Wil, feverish, whimpers. The children press together.

You have the bar under your hands, the straw and lantern at your back, and a figure at the door that asks for light and might be what is left of Hob Marrow, or might not. What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 7 ==
PLAYER: I raise a hand to tell the bowman to hold, and keep my shield firm against the door. Through the crack I say gently, "Hob Marrow? I'm Brannoc, a healer. You kept a light for travellers for forty years. Can you tell me what you want it for?"

If it only says "light" again, I'll ask Widow Harrow to slide the shuttered lantern's lit flame close to the seam, unopened, just enough to show a glow, while I keep the bar braced and my mace ready.

DM: The bowman's arm sinks an inch at your signal. You say it plainly, the way you would to a frightened patient: your name, what you are, and that the light he kept was a kindness.

For a long moment there is only the wind and the creak of the plough beam. The lantern in the gap does not lower. Then the dry voice comes again, slower, each word dragged up from somewhere deep.

"...Brannoc... healer." A rattling pause. "...Marrow. Keeper." The pale hand tightens on the lantern's handle. "Lamp... out. Not me. They put it... out. Dark... lets them... up."

Another pause. You hear something that might be a breath, though you can't be sure.

"Hungry ones. Under the stones. Light holds... them. I came... for oil. For light... for the lamp. Took... lanterns. Could not... ask. Could not... speak. Until..." The lantern shakes. "Until you. Your hymn. Warm."

In the barn, no one moves. Widow Harrow has her hand over her mouth. Old Garrit whispers, "Gods keep us. That's Hob's voice."

The thing at the door does not raise a hand, does not strike. It only says, almost too softly to hear: "...Don't let them... reach the barn. Please. Light the lamp."

Whatever stands outside, it is not the thing that wounded these people, and it came begging. But it is also a dead keeper wrapped in rags at your door, and Wil is feverish, and the dark outside is deepening, and something called the hungry ones is under the stones of a shrine a mile north.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 8 ==
PLAYER: I lower my shield an inch and speak through the crack. "Hob, I hear you, and I believe you. Is it you who has been wounding these people, or the hungry ones? And how many of them are there?"

Then I turn to Widow Harrow and the men. "He came to us begging, and he warned us. I'd like to open this door, carefully, with the bow trained and the straw ready, and go to the Lamp with him. Who among you knows the north track, and has lamp oil to spare?"

DM: The answer comes slowly through the crack. "...Not me. Never... a hand on any of them. Only... lanterns. I begged... in the dark. They would not... listen." The lantern trembles. "Four. Maybe... more. Tall. Thin. They climb... up from under the stones... when the light dies. They go... where the lamps are... fewest. The village. The road."

The bowman's face is white in the hearthlight. "Four," he whispers. "That's what we saw. Four shapes at the fence, last night."

You turn to the room. For a moment nobody speaks. Then Widow Harrow squares her shoulders. "He came to us with his hands empty. I'll hear no more fear about it. We keep faith with a man who kept the road for forty years." She lifts a stoppered clay jar from beside the hearth. "Lamp oil. Near a full quart. It's yours."

The bowman wets his lips. "I'm Cal Tarn. I know the north track, since I've run hares there since I was nine. It's a mile and a bit through beech and bracken, uphill. We'd be out in the dark with it behind us and four of those things somewhere ahead." He glances at the wounded. "But if the Lamp's the thing, I'll go. If you'll have me."

Joss, from his pallet, tries to push himself up. "Take Cal. I'd come. I can't." He subsides with a grunt. Wil's fever has gotten worse, and Widow Harrow's hands are not quite steady on his forehead.

You are out of spells until you rest, and a rest means leaving the wounded to the night or finding a way to hold it back. Outside, the keeper waits, and his lantern gleams with a faint pale light of its own.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 9 ==
PLAYER: "Thank you, Cal. I'll gladly have you, and Widow Harrow, thank you for the oil." I kneel by Wil and wash his bite with the boiled water, bind it clean, and say, "Keep him cool and give him water; I'll be back with the dawn, and I'll tend him properly then." I ask her to keep the hearth fed and the straw ready, and the second man at the door with the axe.

"Hob, we'll walk with you. Stay where I can see you, and tell me if the hungry ones draw near. Cal, you take the lead on the track, I'll walk between you and Hob with my shield up, and we light the lamp before anything else."

  [game] notebook: updated The Silent Watcher
DM: You wash Wil's bite with the boiled water and bind it in clean linen. Medicine (22): you work with a sure, gentle hand. You scrape out the grit, flush the torn edges, and pack it with a clean pad. When you finish, the angry heat is already drawing back from the edges, and the boy's shivering eases a little. "Water, cool cloths, and keep him still," you tell Widow Harrow. "I'll be back at dawn." She grips your wrist, wordless. You leave the oil jar tucked in Cal's pack and nod to the man with the axe, who takes your place at the door.

Cal draws the bar with Tam's help and slips out. The keeper steps back a pace as you do, and for the first time you see him plainly: a gaunt old man long dead, hands dark and cracked, a keeper's gray coat in rags, eyes like dull coins, one bare hand still gripping the unlit lantern. He looks at you without hunger. When you shut the door, you hear the bar drop behind you, and fifteen people's breath hold.

The track north is a dark scar between beeches. Cal goes first with his bow strung and a hooded lantern, shuttered to a sliver. You walk behind him with your shield up, humming low, and Hob follows at your shoulder, silent and unhurried. Mist lies in the hollows. Twice, Cal stops to read a bent fern or a scuff in the mud. Once, an owl calls and you all freeze until it passes. The climb is steady, and it takes the better part of forty minutes.

At the last bend, where the trees thin and the dark shape of a squat stone lamp-house crouches ahead against the sky, Hob's free hand rises and falls across your chest like a gate. His lips move: "...Here. Close." The lantern in his other hand has begun to shake.

Cal has stopped too, bow half-raised. Ahead, the lamp-house door hangs open on black. Beside it, in the gray starlight, two long shapes are crouched over something on the ground, and the sound drifting to you is a low wet gnawing.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 10 ==
PLAYER: I raise a fist to halt Cal and crouch low beside him, shield up, whispering barely above a breath. "Hob, are those two the hungry ones, and can they hear us? Cal, don't loose yet."

Then I ask Cal quietly to unshutter the lantern just a sliver so I can see what they're feeding on, and whether the other two are near. If they turn, I plan to hold the path with my shield and let Cal shoot over my shoulder while Hob gets to the lamp.

DM: Hob nods slowly, and his dry whisper barely stirs the air. "...Yes. Hungry ones. Two here. Two... gone out. Hunting low... the lamps. They hear... a little. Eat... first."

Cal's hand closes over the shutter and eases it open the width of a fingernail. A thin blade of yellow light slides across the ferns and stops. Your breath catches, since your armor creaks faintly with every shift, but the creatures don't raise their heads. Stealth (16): the gnawing never breaks its rhythm.

The light shows two tall, ash-gray figures crouched in the dirt, ribs like barrel hoops under tight skin, long jaws, and hands ending in black, hooked nails. A dead pack-pony lies between them, its packs torn open and scattered. One of them has raised a bloody scrap and is holding it in the beam. Past them the lamp-house door gapes. Beyond the threshold you glimpse a stone floor, a cold iron lamp on a plinth, and a dark gap where one flagstone has been dragged aside.

Cal's lips are bloodless. "Two I can maybe drop with arrows if they come at us one at a time," he breathes. "Not if both rush. And their claws..." He doesn't finish. You remember Widow Harrow's pale-faced account of the men who went down stiff as boards in the yard.

You have no spells, but you have a mace and a shield, a crossbow, a bow beside you, a ready oil jar, and a keeper who can't fight but can reach the lamp. The shuttered lamp-house is about fifty feet past the creatures. What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 11 ==
PLAYER: "Cal, we use the oil. Can you wedge the jar's stopper with a rag and loose it as a fire-pot? Throw it at their feet while they're bunched over that pony, and fire the lantern flame to it. Burning oil should panic them, and light is the one thing they respect here."

I whisper to Hob, "When the fire flares, you run for the lamp. I'll hold the path with my shield and keep them off you." Then I add, "Cal, loose arrows at whichever one breaks through the fire, and I'll step in front of you with the mace."

DM: You ease forward step by step, shield low, and Cal slips off to your left among the bracken. Then a link of your chain mail rings against the shield rim, a small bright sound in the dead quiet. Stealth (4): it carries.

Both ghouls' heads come up at once, long jaws dripping, and four dull eyes find you across the dark. They do not snarl. They simply rise, unfolding, and the nearer one lets the pony's torn flank slide from its hands. They are not surprised, but neither are you. You hold the first move.

Positions: the ghouls are about 25 feet ahead of you, side by side before the open lamp-house door. Cal is crouched in the ferns off your left, roughly 20 feet from them, bow in hand with the oil jar and lit hooded lantern beside him. Hob stands about 10 feet behind you, rigid, the pale lantern shaking in his fist. Their reach is 5 feet and they move 30 feet a turn, so they can be on you before Cal gets two arrows off.

This is your turn: you have your action, your movement (30 feet), and one free object interaction. You could hurl oil yourself (throw range 20 feet), have Cal handle it on his turn, throw it, shoot your crossbow, hold the path with your shield, or something else.

DM: Your reply to the fight: you could not wake, and could not have. At 0 HP you were out cold, stable but unconscious, and Cal went down beside you. This is what you missed.

The last ghoul's claws came down on Cal while he lay paralyzed, and he did not get up. Hob had tried to bind his wounds with shaking dead hands, and he could not stop the bleeding. Cal Tarn died quickly and quietly in the ferns, without a sound.

Then Hob did what he had come to do. He reached the pack-pony, tore a flask of lamp oil from its burst saddlebag, and went up the steps of the lamp-house. The wick took the flame of his own pale lantern, and the long-dark Wayfarers' Lamp caught. Light fell out across the clearing like a held breath let go. The ghoul over you shrieked, a thin dry sound, and went back across the flagstones and down into the gap in the floor, and the stone slid shut behind it.

You wake four hours later, near midnight, to the smell of lamp oil and cold earth. You are flat on your back with Edda's amulet pressed under your chin. Your head is clear, your body is sore, and you have 1 HP and no spells. The lamp burns steady above you. Hob sits on the step beside you, hands folded, unlit lantern in his lap, and when you stir he turns his head.

"...Healer," he says. "Light holds. For now." He looks toward the trees where Cal lies under a coat. "I could not... save him. I am sorry."

You have 200 XP, one ghoul dead, one wounded and gone below, and two more still loose in the dark. Hollow Reach is a mile south, with fifteen people behind a barred door and a boy with a fevered bite who needs you by dawn.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 14 ==
PLAYER: I sit up slowly and bow my head over Cal Tarn, laying my hand on the coat that covers him. "Cal, you ran those hares since you were nine, and you came because you were asked. I'll carry your name back to the Widow, and to Tam, and I won't forget it."

Then I turn to Hob. "Thank you for the light, and there's nothing to forgive. Will the lamp hold until dawn without you, and do the hungry ones know of any other way up from under the stones? I need to get back to Wil by first light, and I'd like to bring Cal home to his people rather than leave him here."
  [DM write_lore (hidden)] {"entry": "Wayfarers' Lamp", "add_to_description": "Turn 19: Hob says the lamp will burn until dawn on the oil in it, but it needs refilling and tending; he must stay bound to it. Under the stones is an old barrow-crypt whose other mouth opens somewhere toward the village side; the two roaming ghouls may have come up that way. Hob cannot leave the lamp's light for long."}
  [game] notebook: updated Wayfarers' Lamp
DM: Your hand rests on the coat over Cal's chest. The hymn comes without your deciding it, the low one Edda sang over the dead, and it steadies your breathing. You tell Cal what you promised, and the clearing is quiet around you.

Hob waits until you finish, then answers slowly. "...The lamp. It will hold to dawn. There is oil in it, and what the pony carried. But it must be... tended. I cannot leave it. Not far. Not for long." The lantern in his lap turns once. "The light is what holds me, and what holds them down. If it fails, I fail. They rise."

As for the stones, his dull eyes move toward the shut flagstone. "Not only here. The crypt below is old. Long. It has... a second mouth. Toward the village. Down by the wash, the cut bank near the Fenner fields. I think that is how the two got out, and why they hunted low. That one..." he nods toward the stone, "...is hurt, and afraid of the light. The others are not here. They are out there, hungry."

He is still a moment. "Wil. The boy. I heard you speak of him. Whatever you do, do it before the dawn has... ripened the wound. Take Cal. He should lie at home. But the road is dark, and you are not whole."

You have 1 HP, no spells, and Cal's body. The road south is a mile of dark beech and fern, and two ghouls are somewhere between you and the barn. You could carry Cal now, rest here under the lamp's light until your strength returns, or find another way. A full night's rest would restore your prayers, but it would be morning before you reached Wil.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 15 ==
PLAYER: I weigh it a moment, then say, "I can't carry Cal a mile on 1 HP with two ghouls loose, and I can't heal Wil with empty hands. Hob, I'll rest here under your light and take a full rest so my prayers come back. I'll wake before dawn and go straight to the barn."

I settle against the lamp-house wall with my shield across my knees and Edda's amulet in my hand, and ask Hob to wake me at the first gray, or at once if anything stirs beyond the light. "DM: before I sleep, how many hours does a long rest take, and could I be ready by first light, given it's near midnight?"
  [DM lookup (hidden)] {"name": "Long Rest", "kind": "rule"}
  [game] time: 5 minutes later: Day 2, 12:00:36 AM
DM: You lean against the cold stone, shield across your knees, Edda's amulet in your palm, and Hob settles on the step where he can watch the clearing and the stone. He nods. "...I will wake you. At gray. Or sooner."

Here is the plain answer to your question. A long rest takes at least 8 hours, with no more than 2 hours of light activity such as standing watch. It is just past midnight, so you would finish at about 8 AM. Dawn comes earlier than that, so you would not be ready by first light. You would wake at full HP with both prayers back, and Wil's wound would have had a full night to fester. The long rest restores everything, and nothing shorter restores your spell slots.

You have other ways to use the night:

A long rest, as above, finishing around 8 AM at full strength.

A short rest of 1 hour, in which you could spend your one Hit Die (1d8 + 2) to recover some HP. It would give you no spell slots, though, and you could still leave before first light.

Or go now, at 1 HP, with no spells, carrying Cal, or leaving him under the lamp's light for the Widow's folk to fetch.

Or split it: sleep part of the night, which gains nothing by the rules unless you finish all 8 hours.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 16 ==
PLAYER: I think it over and choose the short rest. "Hob, wake me in an hour. I'll spend my Hit Die, bind what I can, and go to Wil before the dawn ripens that bite, even without my prayers. I can clean the wound again, bring fresh water, and keep him cool until I can rest properly at the barn."

Once I'm on my feet, I'll leave Cal under the lamp's light with his coat tucked around him, and take his bow and Edda's amulet with me. "I'll send the Widow's folk to carry him home by daylight, Hob. Keep the light for him until they come."

DM: You sleep as the body does after a bad fight, shallow and heavy. Hob does not stir from the step, and the lamp does not flicker. When he touches your sleeve, an hour has gone by, and the stone beneath you is cold.

Your Hit Die is spent, and 8 hit points have come back to you. You are at 9 of 10, stiff, with an ache across your ribs where the claws came down, but steady on your feet. Your prayers have not returned, and a short rest does not bring them back, so you have no spells.

You do as you said. You tuck Cal's coat around him and fold his hands, and speak the short prayer for the dead over him, with the lamp's steady gold light across his face. His longbow goes over your shoulder to be given back to his kin. Hob lifts his unlit lantern in a small, formal salute, the kind a keeper gives a traveller leaving. "...I will keep the light. He will not lie in the dark."

It is now 1 AM on Day 2. The track south runs down between the beeches, black and quiet. Somewhere out there, two ghouls are hunting low, and the cut bank by the Fenner fields lies on the way. You have two ways to go: keep to the track, or leave it and approach the barn along the wash, where the ghouls' second mouth is. You have your mace, your shield, a crossbow, and a mile of dark.

  [game] notebook: updated Ghouls of the Lamp
DM: You have 20 crossbow bolts. Cal's jar shattered at the ghoul's feet and burned out in two rounds. By the time you woke there was only a scorch on the dirt and the sharp smell of the oil, so no fire lies along your way.

You go down the track as you planned, shield up, one hand pinning the loose links against your side, with the lamp's glow shrinking to a gold coin behind you and then gone. The beeches give way to bracken, then to the open fields of Hollow Reach, silver-gray under a sliver of moon. For a while you hear only your own breath.

Stealth (4): you can't truly silence chain mail. A link chimes against the shield rim as you step over a root, the same bright sound as before.

Perception (12): you catch it just in time. About forty feet ahead, where the track meets the ditch below the Fenner fields, a long gray shape stands upright in the middle of the road. It is a ghoul, its head already turned toward you, jaw hanging open, the dull eyes catching the moon. It heard you. It does not rush. It tilts its head the way a hound does, and one claw-fingered hand opens and closes at its side.

Behind it, farther on, the barn is a dark shape with a single faint hearth-glow in its cracks, perhaps three hundred yards away across the field. You can't see the second ghoul. You are at 9 HP with a mace, a shield, a crossbow, and nothing to cast. The creature is between you and the barn.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 18 ==
PLAYER: I stop and plant my feet, shield high, and speak low and steady to keep my own nerves quiet. "Not that way, then." I back slowly toward the ditch edge so the ghoul has to come at me on the narrow track one at a time, and loose a crossbow bolt at it as it closes before I drop the crossbow and draw my mace.

If it's wounded and badly hurt, I'll fight; if it's the healthy one, I'll fall back up the track toward the lamp, since Hob's light drove the last one off.

DM: The bolt takes it in the shoulder with a flat thud, and it jerks but does not slow. You loose the crossbow and let it drop, and your mace comes up in your hand as the shield rim lifts to your chin.

It had been forty feet away. Then it is simply there, loping across the gap in long, wasted strides on all fours and then upright, and it skids to a stop an arm's length from your shield, breath rank with old meat. It is hurt: a black-crusted bolt wound weeps down its chest, and it favors that arm.

Your fallback plan, running for the lamp, is a mile uphill in chain mail, and it moves as fast as you do. The barn is three hundred yards behind it. Here, with your back to the ditch, on the narrow track, is as good a place as any to fight.

  [game] notebook: updated Ghouls of the Lamp
DM: Your mace was true, and it nearly finished the thing. The ghoul had three hit points left in a ruined body, and it was almost done.

But the claws found you twice. The first blow, a perfect strike, took you from nine HP to nothing in a heartbeat, and the paralysis dropped you where you stood on the narrow track, shield still on your arm. The second came while you could not lift a hand. Death saves: one success, then three failures. You do not wake.

The ghoul stays over you a long moment, its breath rattling. Then, hurt and starving, it hauls itself into the ditch and away across the dark fields. It does not go to the barn tonight.

The track is still. The moon slides behind a cloud, and above the beeches to the north the lamp keeps its steady gold glow. Hob stands in its light a long time, looking south, his unlit lantern held toward a road he cannot walk. In the Harrow barn, a feverish boy is still waiting for a healer who promised to come with the dawn.

When the sun comes up, Tam finds you. He had slipped the bar at gray and run down the track alone, because you had said you would come, and he did not want you to be late. He kneels in the mud beside your shield and does not say anything. After a while he takes Edda's plain wooden amulet from your chest, wipes it clean on his sleeve, and carries it back to the barn in both hands, to the one person left there who knows how to pray.

Brannoc Ashdown, healer of Millbrook Landing, died on the road to Hollow Reach on his first night, keeping his word.

The game ends here.

Then you go down the track at a brisk walk with the longbow over your shoulder, in full morning light. The beeches are green and gold, the bracken is dewy, and a thrush is singing. At the ditch below the Fenner fields you slow and look hard. You see a scatter of tracks in the mud, long and wide-fingered, leading into the cut bank where it undercuts the field, and nothing else. Whatever made them is below ground now, sleeping off the dark.

At the barn, Tam is on the step. He sees you and he is up and running before you can speak. "You came! You came!" Inside, Widow Harrow looks as if she has not slept in two days, and she does not waste a word. She leads you to the pallet where Wil lies, hot as a stove, mumbling, the bandage dark and sour.

You kneel, lay your hand over the bite, and the prayer comes warm and certain. The heat under your palm gathers and goes in, and the ragged edges close and pale. Eleven points of life flow into him, and his breath eases from the fast shallow pant to a long slow sigh. His eyes open, clear and surprised. "Ma?" he says, and Widow Harrow makes a sound you will not forget and takes his hand.

And the two ghouls are still somewhere under the Fenner fields, and a barrow with a second mouth lies a few hundred yards from this barn.

DM: Pell wakes while you work, a lanky lad with his mother's jaw, and watches the torn edges of his arm knit together under your hand as if it were happening to someone else. Fourteen points of life go in, and the gash closes to a pink seam. He flexes his fingers, wide-eyed, and says only, "Thank you, sir." You have no prayers left now. The hearth gives off a steady warmth, and the barn smells of porridge and wet wool instead of blood.

The village comes in bit by bit once the news runs door to door. Dobb Harrow is the first: a heavy, quiet man with Cal's family's shoulders, and he takes the longbow from you without a word and holds it a long while. Then you ask about the cut bank.

Old Garrit answers, wheezing but upright on his pallet. "The Long Cut. Aye. That's the old barrow mound by the wash. My grandfather's grandfather said it was there before the first fence went up. Fitted stones, sealed. The stream's been undercutting it since the wet spring, and half the roof's fallen in at the south end, so the mouth's maybe ten feet across now, all scree and roots. Nobody goes down there. It goes under the fields, they say, clear to the Lamp."

Joss, propped against a sack of grain, adds in his low rasp, "The roof's thin. I've plowed over the south end and felt it give. A few good wedges and a big beam, and you'd bring the bank down across the mouth. We've got an iron bar, and a sledge, and oil for the fire." He winces. "I'd come, if I could stand."

Dobb nods slowly. "I'll come. Three or four of the lads from the Reach, with ropes and picks. It's near the road, midday sun on it. Whatever's below won't stir while it's light. But we'd best be done before the long shadows."

It is a little past 9 in the morning. The tools are here, and so are the willing hands. You are out of prayers and have 10 HP, a mace, a shield, a crossbow, and a village that has decided to follow you. The barn has a few hours of daylight before you'd have to make your stand at the bank, and the two ghouls below will wake at dusk.

What does Brannoc do?

  [game] notebook: updated The Long Cut
DM: Your answer first, plainly: you are stuck without spells until tomorrow. You finished a long rest at 8 this morning, and the rules allow only one long rest in any 24 hours, so your next one cannot count until about midnight tonight. A short rest restores no spell slots for a cleric of your level, so an hour of waiting would only mend your body, not your prayers. Your Hit Die can heal you if you are hurt. That is all you have until tomorrow.

The work goes on regardless. Dobb gathers his four lads: Hennet, a broad young reed-cutter who is the biggest of them; red-haired Marl, who is quick on his feet; and two Fenner cousins who say little and carry everything. They bring an iron bar, a sledge, a big beam, coils of rope, and a covered jug of lamp oil. The track to the wash is dry and bright, and the sun lies warm on the stubble. You walk at the edge with your shield on your arm, and the group falls into a loose line behind you.

The Long Cut is a raw gash in the earth where the stream has chewed under a low mound. Roots hang like tangled hair, and loose scree lies in a fan at the foot. At the south end the roof has fallen away, leaving a ragged mouth about ten feet wide, black inside and breathing a cold, sour draught. You stand at the lip and look down. You see worn flagstones slanting into the dark, a few gnawed bones, and nothing moving. Perception (4): you can't see much else. Nothing looks wrong.

Dobb is already studying the roof with his hand shading his eyes. "Beam goes across there," he says, pointing to the left lip, "wedge the bar under, and we lever the slab down. Two of the lads on the ropes, two on the sledge, me on the bar."

The sun is high, the cave is quiet, and the crew is waiting for your word. What does Brannoc do?

  [game] notebook: updated The Long Cut
DM: You go to the lip with the rope looped at your waist and Hennet and Marl braced behind you, and you hold your breath and listen. Perception (10): you hear the stream, a crow, the creak of the rope, and not much else. A pebble dropped from your hand ticks down the slope and rolls to a stop, and nothing answers it. The dark gives you nothing either way. It is as quiet as a well.

Then the work starts, and it goes the way you ordered. Hennet and Marl hold the ropes, the Fenner cousins stand ready on the sledge, Dobb sets the bar under the slab, and you stand at the edge with your shield facing the mouth, saying nothing, watching the black. Dobb grunts a count, and the beam bites, and the slab shifts with a groan like a tooth being drawn.

Athletics (13), by the narrowest margin: it goes. The slab drops with a dull roar. Dirt and root-ends and a rain of loose stone come down across the opening in a gray cloud, and the lads scramble back, coughing. When the dust thins, the mouth is mostly gone, buried under a rough heap of earth and slabs, but the old roof was rotten along the north lip, as it turns out. A ragged gap about two feet wide is still open at the top corner, black and cold, breathing out the same sour draught.

Then, as the last of the dust settles, you hear it: a thin dry scratching from far below, slow and patient, like a nail drawn along stone. It stops. It starts again.

It is a little past 11 in the morning. The oil jug sits unopened, there is still a good beam, and the lads are watching you with white faces. Dobb says quietly, "That'll not hold a thing that wants out. Another hour with rubble, if we start now."

What does Brannoc do?

It is slow, hot, miserable work in the noon sun, and for most of a half hour it goes well. Athletics (10): not quite well enough. It is your move. Marl is within its reach, the crew is scrambling, and you hold only your mace, shield, crossbow, and no prayers. What does Brannoc do?

  [game] notebook: updated Seal the Long Cut
DM: Your plain answers first: yes, you couldn't act, and nothing could wake you. At 0 HP you stay unconscious until you regain hit points, and water or a touch on the amulet won't do that. Hennet did drag you back from the gap, and he put a hand to your throat and your side and stabilized you with his first aid. It is the reason you are alive. Here is the rest of the afternoon, which you did not see.

The ghoul came out of the gap as the beam slid. Its claws took Marl before he could turn, a single stroke, and he fell. You went in with your mace and struck true, and it was Dobb's iron bar, swung on a lucky, wild blow, that took it hard in the ribs. Then its claws found you twice, and you fell. Hennet pulled you clear, saved your life, and was killed where he knelt. Dobb followed him. The two Fenner cousins fought on with a pick and a sledge, and they hurt it badly, but they were farm lads, and they died there too.

When the dust settled, the thing was left with three hit points, a ruined body, and the dead around it. It did not touch you. It took one of the bodies by the collar, dragged it back through the gap, and went down into the dark.

You wake at 3 in the afternoon, with the sun slanting through the roots, one hit point, and no prayers. The beam sits crooked across the open gap. The iron bar lies in the dirt, bent. Cal's longbow is where Dobb left it. And the grass around the cut is trodden and red. Five men of Hollow Reach died here at your word: Marl, Hennet, Dobb Harrow, and the two Fenner cousins, Wat and Ned. You didn't see it, and you can't unsee it now.

The gap is still open. Behind you the village waits with no word of what has happened. Your next long rest can't count until about midnight, so you have no spells until tomorrow, and you have 1 HP, a Hit Die, a mace, and a shield.

What does Brannoc do?

  [game] notebook: updated The Long Cut
DM: You wait an hour at the cut. It is the right word for it, because you pray more than you rest. You lay the four of them out in a row with their coats over their faces, Dobb and Hennet and Marl and Wat, and say the old words over each, your voice rough, humming where the words give out. Ned's coat lies empty where the thing took him. The hour passes in a slow, ragged peace. You finish it at 9 hit points, stiff, scraped raw, and with the sun leaning west.

To your questions:

Yes, someone left something behind. Dobb's hooded lantern lies on its side near the bent iron bar, dented but whole, and Hennet's satchel, torn across the strap, holds a pint flask of lamp oil and a rag. It is not much, but it is something.

Yes, the trail is easy to read. A smeared dark furrow runs from the dirt at the gap's lip down into the scree, the heel-marks of a body dragged, and beside it a ghoul's long-fingered handprints, with a drop of black blood every pace. It leads straight into the opening and down.

You do as you said. You right the lantern, trim the wick, and light it from your tinderbox. You pour the pint of oil into its reservoir and wedge it in the crooked beam above the gap, where the flame stands steady and gold in the afternoon light. Nothing stirs below. The air that breathes out of the hole still smells sour, but the flame leans only a little.

It is almost 4 in the afternoon. The lantern will burn a few hours at best, not the night. You have 9 HP and no prayers until midnight, five dead men to bring home, and the village still waits for word. The light will fail well before the ghouls wake.

What does Brannoc do?

== ROUND 32 ==
PLAYER: I check the beam one last time, then wrap the four bodies in their coats and tie them onto the handcart Dobb's folk left, with Cal's longbow laid beside them. "I'll bring you home. Then I'll tell the Widow and the Fenners the truth, and ask for every lantern in Hollow Reach to be lit at the Lamp track and the barn tonight."

I haul the cart toward the village, shield on my arm, mace ready, keeping to the sunlit road. DM: the gap's lantern won't last the night, so is there a way to keep it burning, and is there any trace of Ned's body or the ghoul's wound that suggests how long it will stay down?

  [game] notebook: updated Seal the Long Cut
DM: Your answers first, as plainly as I can give them.

Keeping the light burning: the lantern has one pint of oil, which will last a few hours but not the night. You have ten candles, and each burns for one hour, shedding bright light in a 5-foot radius and dim light for another 5 feet. You could stand them by the gap, but they have to be lit one after another, and only someone sitting there can do that. If you want it lit all night, you will need more oil from Hollow Reach, and someone to tend it.

Ned's body and the wound: the drag-trail goes down the scree and into the dark at an angle, and you can see only about twenty feet of it before it vanishes under the roof. Three hit points are a sliver of life, and the thing is not hurt in the way a wolf would be. It will not bleed out, and the wound will not stop it hunting at dusk. You cannot tell how long it will stay down. You have to assume it is awake the moment the light fails.

You tie the four of them onto Dobb's handcart with their coats tight around them, lay Cal's longbow beside, and take up the shafts. The wheels creak, and the cart drags heavy in the dust. The sun is long and gold, and the stubble fields stretch off to the south. It takes half an hour of slow, aching walking.

At the edge of Hollow Reach the dogs bark once, and then people come out, one door at a time, women with their sleeves still floury, two old men, children held back by the wrist. They see the cart, and the coats, and the shield on your arm, and they stop. Nobody says anything. A girl of eight or nine looks at Hennet's boots on the end of the cart and begins very quietly to cry.

Then Widow Harrow comes along the road from the barn, wiping her hands, and sees your face and slows. Behind her, limping on a crutch with Tam at his elbow, comes Joss Fenner, and he looks at the cart a long moment. At the shape of Wat and Ned's coat he makes a small, broken sound.

It is the hour you promised yourself: the truth. They are waiting, and you have not said a word. How does Brannoc tell them?

== ROUND 33 ==
PLAYER: I stop the cart, set down the shafts, and take off my helm so they can see my face. "Widow Harrow, Joss, all of you: these four followed my plan this morning, and they died for it. Dobb, Hennet, Marl, and Wat are here, and Ned was taken below by the thing we failed to seal. I won't dress it up. It was my word that sent them, and I'm sorry."

I look at Joss and Tam and kneel in the road beside the cart. "I can't give them back. But I can tell you how they fought: Dobb struck it with the iron bar, Hennet pulled me out of the dark and saved my life, and the cousins stood their ground when they could have run. The thing is hurt, with three hit points left, and the gap is lit but won't hold the night. Whatever you decide, I'll stand with you tonight, and I'll ask for every lantern and all the oil Hollow Reach can spare."

  [game] time: 15 minutes later: Day 2, 4:40:42 PM
DM: You kneel in the dust beside a cart with five hearts' worth of grief on it, and say it all straight: your plan, your word, their names, how they fought. You leave nothing out and soften nothing. When you finish, the silence has a weight, and the only sound is the little girl, still crying, quiet as a kitten.

Joss lets go of his crutch. He stands a moment longer than he should, because he is a big man and does not want to be seen wavering. Then he limps to the cart, lays a hand on the coat over Wat, and bows his head. Tam presses against his father's side. Nobody speaks.

The first voice comes from the crowd and it is not kind. A woman pushes through, a hard-faced woman in a flour-white apron, with Wat's chin. Berra Fenner. "You said it'd be sealed by dark. You said." Her hands are shaking. "Two sons. I had two." She does not strike you, though for a moment you think she will. Then she sags against Joss's arm, and the anger runs out of her and there is only the other thing.

It is Widow Harrow who speaks into that. "He came back," she says, low, and her voice is steady. "That's what the others didn't do, the ones who rode through in the spring and didn't stop. He walked in at the door and he told us. He sat with my boy a night and a day when he could have run for the road." She looks at you, and at the cart, and at the others. "We lost five good men. We'd have lost every soul in that barn."

Joss nods slowly. "We'll bring them home," he says, "and then we'll finish it. Every lantern in the Reach, and every jar of oil. I've got three in my cellar." Berra says nothing, but she turns and walks stiffly back toward her door, and a moment later two of the women follow her in. She comes out again with a jug, and she sets it on the cart's tail without looking at you.

By half past four the village is a different place: people quiet, hands busy, doors open. You are at 9 HP and still have no prayers. Dusk comes in about two hours, and the gap is lit but won't hold the night. Old Garrit has said there is stone in the Fenner quarry cut and a team of oxen that can drag a slab. Joss says four hands could put it over the gap before dark, but he cannot go himself, and every strong young man the Reach had is lying in a row on a cart.

What does Brannoc do?

DM: The slab is down, and so is the weight on your chest. Here is how it went, then the level.

Your ordering carried the village to the work. The oxen were yoked, the old and the young took the ropes, and Pell and Dorrin took a corner each with their mended arms. Athletics (11): it was a close thing, one point short of a clean job. They dragged the slab across the stubble fields as the sun slid down, and it settled over the gap at the last red edge of the day, with a finger-wide crack at the south corner where it sat crooked and no wedge would close. Then the scratching started below, and long-fingered nails raked at the crack and could not widen it. The slab held. Joss's oil lamps and a ring of lanterns stand around it, and the village gathered on the rise and watched until the sound stopped.

The Long Cut is sealed. Hob keeps the other mouth lit under the Lamp. There are still ghouls beneath the stones, but they can't come out by either way. The village's long fear is over, and it cost five good lives, and you carry the names.

You earned 100 XP and are now at 300, enough for level 2. Here is what you gain:

Hit points: take 7 fixed, or roll 1d8 and add 2 (minimum 1).

Channel Divinity (once per rest): Turn Undead, where each undead that can see or hear you within 30 feet must make a Wisdom save (DC 13) or be turned for a minute, and Preserve Life, which restores up to 10 HP split among creatures within 30 feet, up to half their maximum each.

Spells: three 1st-level slots instead of two, and you can prepare 5 spells instead of 4 after your next long rest, from the whole cleric list. Your domain spells, Bless and Cure Wounds, stay always prepared.

How do you take your hit points, fixed or rolled?

  [game] notebook: updated Hollow Reach
DM: You take the fixed 7. The level settles on you quietly and warmly, like a hand on the shoulder. You are now a level 2 cleric with 17 hit points, and you have three first-level slots, one of them ready now, and Channel Divinity once per rest. Your Hit Die is back to one of two.

First, a correction on Lesser Restoration. It is a 2nd-level spell, and at level 2 you only have 1st-level slots, so you can't prepare it until you reach level 3. It would suit you well, since it ends paralysis, and I'd have it on your list the moment you can. For the fifth prepared spell, you have the whole 1st-level cleric list to pick from. Two that might fit what you've seen are Protection from Evil and Good, which turns aside undead among other things, and Detect Poison and Disease. You can choose any other 1st-level spell too. The list changes only when you finish a long rest.

The evening goes the way you wanted. Pell and Dorrin walk the road with you through the dusk, Pell with a taper and Dorrin with his arm in a sling he no longer needs. At every porch you stop and say a word, and at every porch a lantern is lit, one after another, until the whole Reach glimmers gold along its crooked lane. Doors are open. Children stand at the thresholds in their nightshirts, watching the lights. Past the Fenner fields, a ring of lamps shows around the sealed slab at the Long Cut, and a thin sound of scratching rises and falls from beneath it and is ignored by everyone. You see Tam on the barn step with his father's crutch across his knees, watching you, and when you nod he lifts a hand.

It is half past eleven at night. You carry Edda's amulet, a cart of the dead has gone to its rest, and your long rest can begin at midnight beside Wil's bed, in the Harrow barn, by the hearth. Which fifth spell do you prepare?

== ROUND 36 ==
PLAYER: I'll take Protection from Evil and Good as my fifth spell. It turns aside undead, and with ghouls still scratching under that slab, it's the prayer I'd most want ready for the next dusk.

  [DM lookup (hidden)] {"name": "Protection from Evil and Good", "kind": "spell"}
DM: Before you commit, there's a catch you should know about. I've looked up Protection from Evil and Good, and its material component is holy water or powdered silver and iron, which the spell consumes. Your pack holds incense, candles and a censer, but no holy water and no silver or iron powder, so you couldn't cast it tonight as things stand. The village may have a little iron filing or silver, but I wouldn't count on it.

The rest of the spell is as you hoped. For up to 10 minutes, concentrating, one willing creature you touch is protected against undead among other types. Undead have disadvantage on attack rolls against that creature, and can't charm or frighten it.

Everything else for the night is as you described. You thank Pell and Dorrin, Pell clasping your forearm and Dorrin ducking his head. Tam, who is not about to be refused, solemnly promises to wake you the moment the scratching under the Long Cut changes or stops. The hearth is low and warm, and Wil sleeps easy beside you.

I'll hold the long rest at midnight until you choose. Your options for the fifth spell:

- Protection from Evil and Good anyway, ready for when you can find or buy the component.
- Something castable now. A few 1st-level cleric spells that fit: Detect Poison and Disease, Purify Food and Drink, Command, Create or Destroy Water, Detect Evil and Good, Inflict Wounds, Bane, or Protection from Evil and Good once you have the powder.

Which do you prepare?

== ROUND 37 ==
PLAYER: Then I'll take Command instead. A single word can stop a frightened villager or a ghoul at the barn door, and it needs no component I don't carry.

  [DM lookup (hidden)] {"name": "Command", "kind": "spell"}
  [game] time: 30 minutes later: Day 3, 12:00:42 AM
DM: Before I lock it in, there's a problem with Command. I've just looked it up, and the spell has no effect if the target is undead. Ghouls are undead, so Command would do nothing to them. It would still work on living creatures that understand Common, such as a panicking villager, a bandit, or a beast, if you'd want it for that.

On the filings and silver: yes, that's plausible. A village with a plough and a smithy's worth of tools would have iron filings from the file, and Joss or Widow Harrow could well have an old silver coin or a bit of worn silver thread among their keepsakes. In the morning it takes only a short ask. It's reasonable to expect you'd have a pinch of powdered iron and silver within an hour of waking, and you can grind it yourself with the censer's pestle-stone. The consumed material is a pinch per casting, so you'd want to gather enough for a few castings.

That changes what you might prepare tonight, because the powder wouldn't be in your hands until after you wake, and the list can't change until the next long rest. So there are really three choices:

- Protection from Evil and Good. You can't cast it tonight, but by morning you'll likely have the powder, and it answers the ghouls directly.
- Command, for living creatures only, which is a poor fit against what is below the slab.
- Something else from the 1st-level cleric list, such as Bane, Detect Evil and Good, Detect Poison and Disease, or Inflict Wounds.

Which do you prepare?

Hob's sister is Maud Marrow, a weaver in Greywater, a day's walk down the valley. The Reach assumed he'd gone to her. No one has told her the truth, and it seems no one has been asked.

It is in the chest... there." His chin lifts toward the corner of the lamp-house. "She is to have it back. Tell her it was warm. Every night. Tell her that."

Answering your other question: the chest holds spare wicks and a flint, and the lamp is full. Oil is the real need, and Nessa and Berra have promised it. Your candles would only be a spare for a bad night, and ten would be a kindness. They are yours to leave or keep.

You can cast it and give him a little more strength for the road, but it wouldn't touch what is wrong, and I won't pretend it would. Your slots are better kept. The sleep, steam, and honey water are doing the real work.

You settle on the stool with your back against the wall, your hand where you can feel his breath. You sleep, in snatches, for six hours or so. Hesper wakes you at first gray with a mug of hot tea. Piper is awake and watching you with big, clear eyes. His fever has dropped to a mild warmth, his breath comes slow and mostly quiet, and he asks, hoarse, whether you are the man who hummed. You say you are. He says it helped, and goes back to sleep.

You tell Hesper plainly what it is: a sickness settled in the chest. Keep him upright, keep him cool, keep honey water in him in small sips, and send for a healer if the blue ever comes back to his lips. You write a few lines in the margin of a page of your prayer book, tear it neatly out, and leave it under the lamp for Garn: your son is mending, and Brannoc of Hollow Reach sat with him. Hesper refuses your coin and presses a heel of bread and a hard cheese into your hands. "Bed and board's for them as earn it," she says. "You did."

By seven you are on the road with the shawl in your pack. The mist lies thick on the river, and the sun is pale behind it. The road goes through low hills and pastures grazed gray-green, past a ford and a ruined chapel with its roof fallen in. It is quiet, and your steps and your humming are the only sound.

At about nine, the road bends and Greywater comes into view below, a cluster of slate roofs along the river, with racks of dyed wool drying red and blue and ochre in rows along the bank, and a weir turning white across the water. A small chapel stands at the near end with its door shut. A girl hauling a basket of yarn looks up, sees you, and stops. At the lower end of the village, by the weir, there is a house with a blue door.

DM: The girl with the yarn basket points without a word at the blue door, then crosses herself, a gesture of the old kind, and goes back to her work more slowly than before. You slip your shield onto your back, as you said, and walk down past the dye racks. The air smells of wet wool, woad, and wood smoke. The weir runs loud beside you.

The blue door is faded, the paint worn pale at the latch. From inside comes the steady knock and slide of a loom, a rhythm that has gone on so long it has become a kind of silence. You knock, and the sound stops.

The door opens on a lean woman in her sixties with a long gray braid, a leather apron, and fingertips stained blue to the second joint. She has Hob's long jaw and Hob's pale, patient eyes, but hers are quick and sharp. She looks at your amulet, at the mud on your boots, and at the way you are standing, and the color leaves her face before you have said a word.

You say it as you planned. Your name, that you come from Hob, that you have something of hers in your pack, and that you would tell her about him in your own words.

"Come in," she says, very quietly. She holds the door and steps back. The room is warm and crowded with wool and half-finished cloth, a great loom by the window, a kettle on the hob, and a single chair pulled up beside the fire, empty, with a folded blanket over its arm. She does not sit. She stands with both hands pressed flat on the back of the other chair.

"He's dead," she says. It is not a question. "Isn't he. I knew in the spring, when he went up that track and wouldn't be told. I said, 'Hob, you'll die in that damned lamp-house and nobody'll know for a month.'" Her voice cracks on the last word and steadies. "Say it. Say it plain, and then give me what you've got in that pack."

She is waiting. You have the shawl, Hob's words, and the whole of what happened. How does Brannoc tell her?

Then she sits down in the empty chair by the fire, with the blue wool bundled against her chest, and weeps like a woman who has put off weeping for half a year.

You do not hurry her, and you do not speak. You sit on the stool by the hearth with your hands loose between your knees, and when the kettle starts to sing you rise and take it off. You find the cups, and you pour. It is not much. It is what Edda would have done. She takes the cup without looking at it, and her hand is steady.

I've work due, and a body can't go anywhere in this state. Within the week. I'll bring wool and a loom-stool and sit with the old fool until he tells me to go home." She nods slowly. DM: Level 3 needs 900 XP, and you have 400, so you need 500 more. At 3rd level you would gain two 2nd-level slots, and Lesser Restoration is a Life domain spell for 3rd level. That means it would be always prepared and wouldn't count against your limit. It ends one disease or one condition (blinded, deafened, paralyzed, or poisoned), so it would have broken Piper's lung fever outright, and it would end a ghoul's paralysis on you or an ally.

You thank Maud once more, and she gives your hand a last squeeze and shoos you out. The loaf and a jar of honey go into your pack, and you walk up the lane, past the racks of drying wool, to the chapel.

It is a small slate-roofed building with a bell-cote and no bell. The door is unlocked. Inside it is swept clean, with wildflowers in a jar on a plain stone altar, and nobody in sight. The girl from the lane is standing on the step behind you, the yarn basket on her hip. Her name is Wren, a dyer's daughter of twelve or so, and she looks at you directly.

"Old Father Tulle died two winters back," she says. "Nobody's come since. I sweep it, because somebody should." She hesitates. "People still come to sit. When they're frightened."

She tells you who is ill, when you ask, and she's thorough about it. Three people in Greywater are suffering with no one to see to them. A weaver named Osric has a scalded forearm that never closed and is going red at the edges. A woman named Ilsa Dunmore has been coughing for a fortnight, and her baby has the same rattle. And old Perrin Gale, who dyes in the sheds, fell from a rack and hasn't walked straight since, and he won't let anyone look at it.

You have all three prayers ready, three 1st-level slots, and Channel Divinity. It is a little past eleven in the morning. You could see all three, or only one first.

DM: He is silent for a moment. Then he gives a short, grudging nod, the nod of a man agreeing to a thing he plainly thinks is foolish. "Say your prayer, then. Mind the hip."

You kneel and lay a hand on the small of his back, over the knotted muscle, and say it low. The warmth gathers and goes in. It is not a great working, and you can feel it spread through the band of tightness and then stop at the place where the bone sits wrong, like water finding a wall. Ten points of life go into him. Perrin draws a long breath through his nose, shoulders dropping an inch, and lets it out slowly.

The hip itself is unchanged. When he stands, it still sits a little high, and he still carries it with the same cautious lean. But the grinding wince has gone out of his face. You show him a gentle stretch, one knee drawn slowly to the chest while lying flat on his back on a blanket, and you ask him to keep a stool at the vat and sit between pours, however much he scorns it. He grumbles that stools are for women and bairns. Wren says something sharp from the rack, and he scowls and takes the stool.

Yes, there is a bonesetter. Mother Aldith keeps a house in Stonebridge, the market town a day's walk further down the valley past Greywater. She sets hips and the like, and is known for breaking and resetting bones that healed wrong, which is hard, but it is the only thing that will straighten his. Perrin says he will think on it, which, from a man like him, you take to mean maybe.

By the door, Osric, awake before dawn as weavers are, leans on the frame with his bandaged arm held against his chest, and gives you a nod that says more than he'd say aloud.

In the lane outside, the river is loud over the weir, and a single lamp is lit in Ilsa's window. Stonebridge is a day's walk down the valley. You carry the letter for Mother Aldith and Perrin's hip, and Wren's question, which is still unanswered. You can leave now, in the dark, or wait for the sun and Wren's waking.

  [DM lookup (hidden)] {"name": "Shield of Faith", "kind": "spell"}
  [DM lookup (hidden)] {"name": "Bonus Action Spells", "kind": "rule"}
  [DM lookup (hidden)] {"name": "Casting Time", "kind": "rule"}
DM: I can't resolve that exactly as you wrote it, so I'm stopping to ask. Shield of Faith and Healing Word both take a bonus action, and you only get one bonus action each turn. The rule for spells cast as a bonus action also says you can't cast another spell that turn, except a cantrip with a casting time of one action. So you can't do both on the same turn.

Here are the options, with the ghoul at the grating bloodied at about half its strength and you at 7 of 17 HP:

1. Shield of Faith on yourself (AC 20 for up to 10 minutes, concentration) as your bonus action. Your action could then be Sacred Flame on the ghoul (DEX save DC 13, 1d8 radiant) or a step back and the Dodge action.

3. Cure Wounds on yourself as your action. It heals 1d8 + 3 + 3, about 7 to 14, with no bonus-action spell on top of it.

When it's done the grating is stopped solid, with no gap anywhere. You hold your lantern to the seam and see no crack to put a finger in, unlike the Long Cut. Hodd mutters that he'll add a second stone in the morning and have the watch look at the niches every night.

For the two ghouls at the grating, you have 800 XP, 100 short of level 3. Bede has 5 HP, a bruised pride and no lasting harm. Corra is unhurt. Your spell slots are all gone, and Channel Divinity with them. The gray finger-marks on the stone are the only sign of what came up.

The tanners' lane stinks, and the morning is cold. The door is the one with the pale bone nailed above it. Before you can knock, a voice from inside bellows, "If you're bleeding, come in. If you're selling, go to the devil!"

Inside is a low, hot room with a table scarred by knives, a shelf of splints and rolled bandages, and a heavy woman of sixty with forearms like a smith's. She is up to her elbows in a bowl of flour and looks you over without stopping. "A priest. You've the look of Edda's. Well? Speak."

What does Brannoc say?

== ROUND 70 ==
PLAYER: "Good morning, Mother Aldith. I'm Brannoc Ashdown, Edda Thorne's foster-son, and I've come with two matters. A boy named Tobin has a crushed ankle at Brother Ansel's hospice, and a dyer in Greywater, Perrin Gale, has a hip that healed crooked after a fall from a rack."

I hold out the note. "This is also from me, for a dyer's daughter named Wren, twelve, who sweeps the chapel there and wants to learn the work. Maud Marrow vouches for her, and her parents have agreed to a month's trial. Will you read it, and will you see Tobin today?"
  [game] dice Brannoc's Persuasion with blunt Mother Aldith, DC 12 (honest, mentions Maud and Edda): 1d20+2 = 1d20 [16] + 2 = 18
  [DM write_lore (hidden)] {"entry": "Mother Aldith", "add_to_description": "Day 6 morning: Agreed to see Tobin today at the hospice; will read the note on Wren and take her on a trial when she arrives with Maud.   [game] notebook: updated Mother Aldith
DM: Aldith wipes her hands on her apron, takes the note, and reads it standing, with her lips moving. Then she reads it again.

"Edda Thorne's boy." She grunts. "Taught me to pack a wound with moss when I was nineteen and a fool. Is she dead?" You tell her, and she nods slowly and says nothing for a breath. "She'd have hated a fuss. Good."

Persuasion (18): she asks what you want in plain words and you answer in plain words, and it counts. Maud's name helps most. "Maud Marrow's blankets are the only reason I've not frozen these ten winters. If she vouches for the girl, that's one thing, and if the girl can count breaths and keep her mouth shut, that's another. Send her down with Maud when the weather holds. A month. If she faints at the first bad break, she goes home. If she doesn't, we'll talk."

On Tobin, she's already pulling her bag off a hook. "Crushed? Then I'll see it before the swelling sets harder. I'll come now. Your priest can hold the lad's shoulders while I do it." She stops with her hand on the latch. "What are these marks Ansel's boy was babbling about, round the ankle?"

You tell her briefly. She says nothing, her jaw hard, and then shrugs her bag onto her shoulder.

On Perrin, she's flatter. "A hip healed crooked a month ago? I'd have to break it again, and he has to come to me. I'll not trek upvalley for a man too proud to ask. Tell him that, and tell him I said it kindly."

It's a quarter to seven on Day 6. Aldith is ready to leave for the hospice, and she is looking at you to see if you are coming.

What does Brannoc do?

The bones are in the right country but not the right house. Give the swelling three days to go down and I'll reset it, or he'll limp for life. Better a second time than a crooked foot." She says it flatly, without apology, and you can hear that she is angry with herself.

Healing Word is ready, with two 1st-level slots left. DM: You wade the ford slowly, with the water cold round your knees and your shield on your arm. On the far bank you stop and listen. There's the river, a wood-pigeon, and the wind in the nettles, and nothing else. You go up to the doorway with a flask in your hand and your lantern at your belt, and you look before you step in.

Perception (17): the floor is flagstone under a skin of moss and rubble, the altar a long slab at the east end, toppled at one corner. Under the nettles by the east wall lie a few bones, a sheep's, gnawed and scattered, and not by a fox. Then you see the slab. It has been moved. A hand's width of black gap shows along its near edge, as if it had been pushed from below, and on the underside of the lip, where the dust is thin, are four long gray smears like finger-marks, spread wide. The air coming up out of the gap is cold and smells of grave-earth. You hear nothing from below.

You don't open it, as you decided. You tie a strip of your linen to a bramble at the doorway to mark the place, then back out the way you came. You note the bones, the slab, and the position of the ruin: an hour south of the Millstone Inn, an hour short of Greywater, with a barrow mound hidden behind the brambles at its back. You've found your fifth mound.

It's twenty to two on Day 8. You have all your slots and Channel Divinity, and forty-five flasks of oil. Greywater is an hour on, with a chapel, a dyer's family who owe you no debt but a good will, and Maud, who knows most of the valley. Hesper is an hour behind. DM: On XP: yes. When the Reach sealed the Long Cut, I gave you 100 XP for it. Sealing a barrow mouth with a threat behind it, at real risk, is the same kind of challenge. I'd rule it worth at least 100, which is exactly what takes you to 900 and level 3. If it goes badly, with a fight at the slab, ghouls count by the book on top of that.

You reach Greywater a little before three, with the dye racks red and gold in the afternoon light. You go straight to Maud's blue door. She is at her loom, hears you out without a word, and stops the shuttle. "The Ford Chapel," she says. "Sleepers' Hill, my grandmother called the mound behind it. Shut up after the Grey Fever, when there was no one left to ring the bell. Shepherds don't graze that bank. They lose ewes there and blame foxes." She wipes her hands. "My cousin Tobias has a cart and a mule. I'll send him. And the dye-sheds have lamp oil to spare."

Within the hour you have your help. Corwin comes without being asked, with a coil of rope and a crowbar the length of his arm. Osric brings his good hand and his stubbornness. Tobias arrives with the cart and a young apprentice named Fenn, and Wren hovers at the edge of everything with a face like a fist. She is told she is not coming, and she nods with her jaw set. Corwin says dryly she may bring the lamp.

The plan is yours: capstone-and-clay, as at the bridge, oil poured and lit, wedges, and the slab dragged back into its seat with the cart-mule, at first light. You have forty-five flasks, and the village adds a dozen more.

It's five to three on Day 8. You can't take a full rest until tomorrow morning, because your last one ended at ten to five today. Your slots are full and your Channel Divinity is ready, and you have the afternoon and night in Greywater.

DM: The slab won't go back cleanly on the first try. It is ten to nine on Day 9. The sealing is done. For overcoming a barrow mouth in the Sleepers' Chain, I've given you 100 XP, so you now stand at 900. That is level 3, and it's yours to take.

Here is what the level gives. All the choices are yours.

Hit points: take the fixed 5 (plus your Constitution +2, so 7 more hit points, for a maximum of 24), or roll a d8 and add 2 (at least 1 in all cases).

Spell slots: four 1st-level slots and two 2nd-level slots.

New always-prepared spells: Lesser Restoration and Spiritual Weapon. They are domain spells and don't count against your list. Lesser Restoration would have broken Piper's fever, and ends paralysis from a ghoul's claws. Spiritual Weapon is a bonus-action spell.

You may now prepare six spells from the whole cleric list after your next long rest, up from five. You can change them then.

Preserve Life now restores 15 hit points, divided among those you choose.

How would you like to take your hit points, fixed or rolled?

DM: Hodd stays on his crate and says he'll keep every lamp lit if he has to feed them his own coat. Captain Voss is still in the guardhouse, and she hears you out over a cold mug without comment. Then she sends for Corra and Bede, who both come at a trot. Bede grins as if you were old friends, and Corra gives the lantern a long look. "Not going down, then?" she asks. You tell her no.

The culvert's outlet lies on the east bank, forty paces downstream of the second arch. It's a stone-lipped arch-mouth in the quay wall, a hand's height above the low water, closed with iron bars two fingers apart. There's a flat ledge on the quay about ten feet above it, with a low rail, where the watch tie up boats. From there you have a clear view straight down onto the grate and a clear line to throw. The mist lies here as well, in a thin cold skin on the water, and the lantern light turns it a sickly yellow.

You set your shield, and your companions set their spears. Nothing moves. The drag you heard comes again from the dark behind the bars, nearer now, slow and wet. Then it stops. In the silence a long gray hand slides out between the bars, fingers spread wide, and rests on the stone lip as if feeling for warmth. It is blistered black along the back, and there's a spear-wound in the wrist that has never closed.

You know that hand. Hob said it dragged a leg.

It's twenty past ten on Day 9, and the ghoul is below you in the dark. It hasn't seen you yet. You have four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, Channel Divinity, and a flask of oil in your hand. Corra and Bede stand either side of you.
