DM: Daylight first: it's the middle of autumn, so about nine hours of good light remain, with dusk at roughly half past five. You've time to look, but not to dawdle.

On the walk Voss answers the second question without slowing her limp. The hill is the old plague-ground, she says, where the town buried its dead in the Grey Fever years, and no one has used it since. Her grandmother called it Stair-Wife's Hill and told the children to stay off it after dark. "I thought it was to keep us from the drop-off by the quarry," she says, flatly.

The hill is a long green swell north of the wall, with rooks on the crown and a low kerb of tumbled stone round its foot. You walk the base slowly with the three of them behind you, and you leave no stone unlooked at.

Perception (15): on the east flank, half hidden in nettles behind the kerb, is a low door-slab set into the hillside. It stands ajar by about a forearm's width, pushed out from within. The earth in front of it is scuffed and trampled, and long drag-marks run through the grass, downhill toward the town and the river, in the line of the culvert. There are old gray finger-smears on the slab's inner edge. Cold air breathes out of the gap and the smell of grave-earth with it. No oil-flasks, no lamps. You hear a slow, dry sound inside, like something shifting on loose stone.

Voss has gone very still. "That's no tale, then." Her hand rests on her sword hilt.

It's a quarter to nine on Day 10. You have four 1st-level and two 2nd-level slots, Channel Divinity, Protection from Evil and Good with its powder, and thirty-eight flasks of oil. Corra holds a rope, and Bede has a halberd. The door is ajar, and the sun is full on the hillside.

  [game] notebook: updated Reedholm
DM: Your first question: yes, probably. Lesser Restoration touches a creature and ends one disease or one of four conditions: blinded, deafened, paralyzed, or poisoned. A marsh-fever is a disease, and it would end it. But it ends one disease on one creature per casting, and you have only two 2nd-level slots a day. If the whole village is sick, you can't cast your way through it. You'd have to use your Medicine to find who is worst off and who simply needs rest, water and a clean bed. It wouldn't touch what the fever left behind, such as weakness or a ruined lung.

You spend the afternoon and evening on errands. At the market you buy fifty feet of hempen rope (1 gp), a hooded lantern (5 gp, burns six hours on a flask, and you can lower the hood to dim it), and five more candles. That's 6 gp, and you're left with 4. Tobin shows you his ankle and asks about the hill. Rudd is up on his crutch complaining about the porridge. You sit with Imma's father at the cart-yard for a while, and ask the carters what you meant to.

You learn this. The river road south forks at a wooden milestone, the Eel Fork. The east branch goes to Reedholm along a causeway, two days on foot, with a ferry-rope at the Black Ford, which a boy keeps. The lights over the drowned barrow are pale green-white and seen on moonless nights, and the reed-cutters call them the Wick-fires. A boy named Sedge brought the first fever cases to market last week and went home sick. Voss, when you ask her, has nothing more. Nobody on her watch has been south of the Eel Fork this month.

You sleep late, from nearly midnight, under the shrine lamp. The long rest ends at twenty to eight on Day 11. You wake with 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Channel Divinity ready. Your prepared list is unchanged. This is a living woman with the marsh-fever, and a bad case.

You get her to the fire and onto the bench with the blanket round her shoulders. She gives her name between coughs: Marta Quill, a reed-cutter's wife from Reedholm. She slept in this hut once before, she says, under the bench, on the way out. She tells you about the lights. "They began when the reeds were cut by the old mound. The Hollow Isle, from the landing: a mile out across the fen, a low reed-ringed hump about forty paces long, grey-green in the mist, with a grey standing stone fallen at its west end. Perception (22): the reeds on the south tip are cut back in a raw swathe where the cutters worked, and a skiff's bow-mark runs in the mud there. On the west flank is a dark gap, low and square, like a door half sunk in the earth. And there are the Wick-fires: three pale green-white glows hanging low over the west side, still and steady even in daylight. They don't flicker like fire or drift like gas. The reeds near them are grey, as if all colour had been drawn out.

It's five to four on Day 14. The trip is about forty minutes each way by skiff, so there's time to scout the south tip and return before dusk, which falls near half past five. You have four 1st-level slots, no 2nd-level slots, and Channel Divinity. Wick and Gorm wait at the staithe.

You look hard for Jory's skiff, and your Perception is a natural 1, a 4 in all. The mist lies thick on the water and the reeds are crowded and grey. You can't make out the shore at all. There might be a skiff tied up on that raw cut or there might not, and you can't swear which. Wick says quietly that the mark in the mud was fresh a week ago. Gorm says nothing and grips his pole.

The Wick-fires do not move. All three hold steady over the west side, pale and low and silent, and the cold that rolls off the Isle is a damp, unhealthy cold. A reed-cutter's nerve is thin here. When the light begins to fade you keep your word, call the turn, and don't land. You are back at the staithe by twenty past five, with the sun gone orange and the village chimneys lit.

You know the Isle's shape now, the cut reeds, the gap on the west, and the three lights, and not whether Jory is alive. You have four 1st-level slots, no 2nd-level slots, and Channel Divinity. You can't rest again until tomorrow, at the earliest a quarter past three in the afternoon. Ode meets you at the landing, her pole-hook in her hand.

  [game] notebook: updated Ode Rush
DM: Your slots first, and it's an answer you won't like. Your 2nd-level slots come back only when you finish a long rest, and the rules allow only one in 24 hours. Your last one finished at half past three this afternoon, so the next can finish no earlier than half past three tomorrow afternoon. A night's sleep tonight, ending near dawn, won't restore them, though your hit points and 1st-level slots are full already.

That leaves you two honest choices. You can land at first light tomorrow with four 1st-level slots, Channel Divinity, and no 2nd-level slots. That means no Lesser Restoration if the dead mound's chill takes anyone, and no Spiritual Weapon. Or you can start a long rest at half past seven tomorrow, finish at half past three in the afternoon with everything restored, and land around half past four with about an hour of light left. The cost of waiting is a night and a day more for Jory, if he lives, and a daylight window that is shorter than you'd like.

Yes, Wick and Gorm can both stay with the skiff while you land. They'd rather, in truth. Wick grips his pole and says he'll keep her off the reeds and ready to run, and Gorm nods, relieved.

Ode takes your honest report with a grim nod. She does not blame you for turning back, and she will keep the village fires high and a watcher at the landing. Down the walks the lamps come on one by one. Marta brings you a bowl of eel stew and sits with you on the staithe a moment without speaking. The three pale glows hang over the west side of the Isle in the dusk.

It's twenty to six on Day 14. You have four 1st-level slots, no 2nd-level slots, and Channel Divinity.

Wick, who is quicker, hears more. He stiffens, one hand raised. From somewhere to the west, thin and faint, comes the bright bone-note of a whistle: three short blasts, and then silence.

"Jory," Wick breathes. Gorm crosses himself the old way.

And the thin dawn light shows you the south tip: the raw swathe of cut reeds, and in it, tarred green with a blue eel painted on the bow, a skiff drawn up on the mud, tied to a stake. No one is in it, and no one is near it. The three pale Wick-fires hang over the Isle's west side as before, steady and quiet. The ground rises behind the cut, a low grassy hump, and a faint trail of trampled reeds runs west along the shore toward the dark gap you saw from the landing.

It's ten past seven on Day 15. You have four 1st-level slots, no 2nd-level slots, and Channel Divinity. The whistle sounded from the west.

  [game] notebook: updated Jory Rush
DM: You step onto the mud beside the green skiff. Wick and Gorm hold the boat, and the rope pays out behind you. You cup your hands and call low across the reeds: "Jory Rush! It's Brannoc, the healer from Reedholm. Blow once if you can hear me, and stay where you are."

For a long breath there is only dripping. Then one thin bone-note comes back from the west, a single blast, weaker than the three before. Wick whispers, "That's him. That's his whistle."

You kneel at the trail. Survival (21): the prints belong to one person, a young man in cut-down boots. The left foot drags a little at the toe. Two sets of prints overlap, one going out and one coming back, and the coming-back set is the shorter, stumbling stride of someone hurt or exhausted. They run west along the shore, keeping to the trampled reeds, and every one of them bends toward the dark gap. You see no other tracks, bare or booted, and no drag-marks.

The reeds along the trail are grey and brittle, drained of colour. The cold is stronger here, and the air has a faint grave-earth smell. The three pale Wick-fires hang over the west side, still and silent.

It's twenty past seven on Day 15. You stand at the edge of the cut reeds with shield up and mace ready. The rope is tied off to the skiff behind you, and you have four 1st-level slots and Channel Divinity.

== ROUND 129 ==

DM: how far is the burned mud-flat from here, and do wisps fear fire, or does it only feed their light?
DM: I'll stop before I roll anything, because your plan has a snag and you should know it first.

The burned mud-flat lies on the near side of Reedholm, well away from the stilts. By pole it's about twenty minutes from here, which is a little under 200 rounds. The village fires are another few minutes beyond.

Wisps don't fear fire. Their stat block gives them resistance to fire, and they fly, so a wall of burning oil on the water wouldn't block them. The one on your tail would simply float over it. Burning oil does the wisp little harm and the skiff some risk, and it wouldn't feed their light. What would help is distance and numbers. A crowd of spears wouldn't help much either, since a wisp is hard to hit and resists ordinary blades and bolts. A mace is nonmagical. What could hurt them is Sacred Flame, since radiant isn't one of their resistances, though they dodge it well. Your Guiding Bolt would hit hard if it landed, since it's radiant too, but you've only one slot left.

You could still pour the oil and light it as a gesture. It would cost you your action this round and do next to nothing to the wisp. Or you could do something else with the action.

It's round 10, and it's still your turn. You have 12 of 24 hit points, one 1st-level slot, and no Channel Divinity. What does Brannoc do?

DM: First your question: no. Hit Dice are spent only at the end of a short rest, which is an hour of quiet. They can't be used in a fight or on a pitching boat. At 1 hit point you're alive, and nothing on the boat can add to that.

DM: You hunch over Jory and Gorm with the shield wide and call to Wick to pole. All three wisps come in together, and all three miss. One bolt goes high, one skitters off the shield, and one curdles against the ward in a hiss of sparks. You are still standing at 1 hit point.

To your question: yes, you would get death saves, but not before the danger. At 0 hit points you fall unconscious and begin death saves, which you roll at the start of each of your own turns. A will-o'-wisp's Consume Life is a bonus action on its own turn. If you're at 0 and it is within 5 feet, it can force a DC 10 Constitution save. On a failure you die outright, and it heals itself. Your +2 Constitution makes that save hard to miss, but it isn't impossible. A hit on an unconscious creature within 5 feet is also a critical hit, which counts as two failed death saves.

So the danger is real. You have no slots and no Channel Divinity. Protection from Evil and Good has about eight minutes. Behind the reeds on the water ahead, a faint line of smoke rises where Reedholm's fires burn. It's round 16, and it's your turn.

What does Brannoc do?

DM: Wick poles the skiff with his teeth set and his eyes wet, and Gorm lies over Jory the whole way, as you told him. The three wisps follow to the edge of the village. Then the fires on the landing flare up, and Ode Rush stands at the staithe with her pole-hook and half of Reedholm behind her, with torches. The lights hang there a long moment. Then they turn and drift back across the water toward the Isle, and the fen is only mist.

They lift you out of the boat. Ode doesn't speak. She kneels and closes your eyes herself, and puts her scarred hand on your forehead for a moment, the way she did with Sedge. Jory, grey and shaking in his blanket, says only, "He came for me." Marta cries without sound. Sedge, who has been out of bed for two days, brings the bowl of broth you never got to eat and sets it by your hand, then does not know what to do and stands there.

They bury you on the dry rise above the Long House, where the first light comes. Ode says the words, and they are plain ones. Reedholm says your name as one of its own. Nobody takes a coin for anything that day. By the third week the thatch is new, the wells are boiled, the last of the sick are on their feet, and Bram Hale and Dessa Pike carry the stones for your cairn. Wick and Gorm go back to the Isle with half the village in daylight, with salt and fire and the old rites for the dead. They find the gap, and what they find in it is theirs to tell. Reedholm sends word to Stonebridge.

The word reaches Brother Ansel in nine days. He sits a long time with the letter in the hospice garden, then writes your name in the book of the faithful where it belongs. He sends riders to Greywater and Hollow Reach. Edda's wooden amulet came back from Reedholm in Marta's hands, wrapped in your blanket. Ansel turns it over for a night. Then he walks to Mother Aldith's with it and puts it in the hands of the girl who holds the lamp steady.

Wren is sitting on her stool by a bed when he comes in. She does not cry at first. She holds the amulet and asks whether it hurts to wear. Ansel says it did not hurt Edda, and it did not hurt Brannoc. She puts it on over her apron. Aldith, who has an opinion on everything, says nothing at all. The next morning Wren is up before dawn, counting breaths at a sick child's cot, humming a tune she doesn't know the words to.

The news goes up the valley. Maud Marrow hears it in Greywater and sits at her loom without weaving. Then she walks up the road to the Wayfarers' Lamp with a new blue shawl and her brother's old flint. Pell and Dorrin Harrow keep the rota there, and Pell has a good arm again and Dorrin a warm back. They say your name when they light the lamp. Hob Marrow, in his quiet, tells them he heard it already. He says the light was lit, and that is all he asks of any man. The lamp does not go dark.

And in a green-hulled skiff on the fen, a boy of nineteen blows his bone whistle once at dawn. He has stopped calling anything a dare. Jory Rush now learns the healing herbs from Marta Quill.

That's the end of Brannoc's story. He went out as he promised he would, and left nobody to suffer alone. Thank you for playing him so well.

**Tamsin Vale, human druid 3 (Circle of the Land, swamp)**
- Abilities: STR 13, DEX 14, CON 15, INT 11, WIS 16, CHA 9. These are the standard array (15, 14, 13, 12, 10, 8) with human's +1 to each.
- Hit points: 24. That's 10 at 1st level (8 + CON 2) and 7 each at 2nd and 3rd, taking the fixed 5 + 2, as we did for Brannoc. She has 3d8 Hit Dice.
- AC: 15. Leather armor is 11, plus DEX 2, plus the wooden shield's 2. Druids won't wear metal, so wood fits. Speed 30.
- Proficiency +2. Saving throws: INT and WIS, so INT +0, WIS +5. Skills: Nature +2 and Survival +5, from the druid list, plus Perception +3 by my ruling for a ferrier. By another ruling she's proficient with water vehicles.
- Spells: save DC 13, spell attack +5. She has four 1st-level and two 2nd-level slots. Cantrips are Guidance, Shillelagh, and Druidcraft (the circle's bonus cantrip).
- Always prepared from the swamp circle, and free: Acid Arrow and Darkness.
- She can prepare WIS 3 + level 3 = 6 spells of your choice. You've named Cure Wounds, Entangle, Goodberry and Fog Cloud. You can choose two more. I'd suggest Healing Word (a ranged fix for a bleeding friend) and Thunderwave, or Speak with Animals, since it's a ritual. You can swap them after any long rest.
- Wild Shape: twice per short rest, beasts of CR 1/4 or less with no flying or swimming. Natural Recovery: once a day on a short rest she can regain slots totalling up to 2 levels.
- Weapons: ferry pole (quarterstaff), +3 to hit, 1d6+1 (1d8+1 two-handed). With Shillelagh she uses WIS: +5, 1d8+3 magical. Sling: +4, 1d4+2.
- Gear: leather armor, wooden shield, ferry pole, sling and bullets, ferry-rope, explorer's pack, druidic focus, herbalism kit, 10 gp.

Which two spells would you like, or shall I use my suggestions? When you've told me, I'll open the story. It's a damp grey dusk, years after Brannoc, and Tamsin is walking the old river road toward Reedholm, with a green-white light in a dream she can't shake.

  [DM write_lore (hidden)] {"entry": "Pim Tarr", "add_to_description": "Years after Brannoc's death: a grown man of about twenty-five, still keeps the ferry rope at the Black Ford, with a bigger punt, a proper hut and a dog. Wary of strangers; remembers Brannoc and the apple slices."}
  [game] notebook: updated Pim Tarr
DM: It has rained all afternoon, and now the rain has thinned to a cold drizzle that beads on your leather and your pole. The old river road is more track than road here, a raised bank of packed clay and cut reed running through flat, grey fenland. Water lies on both sides, black and still. The reeds hiss in the wind, and the light is going the colour of old pewter.

You have walked three weeks from the far south, and you've said perhaps forty words to anyone since the last market. The dream that brought you hasn't changed. In it, pale green-white lights hang low over water, and under them is a drowned stone you have never seen. You woke sweating from it nine nights running, and on the tenth you took your pole and walked north.

Ahead, a channel of slow black water cuts across the road, perhaps forty paces wide. A ferry-rope is strung from post to post. You notice, as a ferrier would, that the rope is well kept, newly tarred, and the posts are sunk deep. A flat punt, broad and clean, is moored on the near bank beside a low reed-and-timber hut with smoke rising from its chimney. A big grey dog lies on the doorstep with its head on its paws, and it lifts its eyes to you but not its body.

A man of about twenty-five sits on the punt's edge, mending a net by the last of the light. He is lean and sun-browned, and he has seen you well before you were close. He sets the net down slowly and rises with one hand resting on the rope. "Road's wet," he says. "Ferry's a copper a head after dark, two if you want the lantern."

Behind him on the far bank, small and low in the dusk, a few lights are lit among the reeds, yellow ones, the warm kind that come from windows. They are not green or white.

You have a full complement of slots and 24 of 24 hit points. The pole is in your hand, and the dog is watching you.

What does Tamsin do?

== ROUND 143 ==
PLAYER: I stop at the edge of the bank and look over the rope and the punt before I look at him. "Good rope. Whoever tars it knows the work." I dig two coppers out of my pouch and hold them out. "I'll take the lantern, and I'll pole if you want a second pair of hands. I was a ferrier myself, down south."

I ask, dry and unhurried, "What's the village across the water called, and is there anyone there who'd know about a drowned stone and lights over the water?"

DM: You stop at the edge of the bank and look at the rope before you look at him. You run your eye along the tar, the knots and the depth of the posts. "Good rope. Whoever tars it knows the work."

Something in his shoulders loosens. He takes the coppers and nods once, and the dog on the step lets its head drop. "I do," he says. "Learned it from a man who learned it before I was born." He looks at the pole in your hand, then at the way you stand with your weight low, and almost smiles. "Southern ferrier, you said. You'd know how to read this water, then. It looks slack and it isn't. There's a pull under the middle at ebb."

You take the stern pole and he takes the rope. The channel is as he says, quiet on top and strong below, and you feel it in your wrists at the midpoint, a slow steady drag toward the west. He watches you correct for it without being told, and he says nothing, which from a man like him is respect. The lantern hangs from the bow-post, yellow and small, throwing a ring of light onto the black water.

Midway, you ask your questions. The village across the water is Reedholm, he says. "Reed-cutters, eel-trappers, and a landing that gets busier every year." Then you say the words "drowned stone" and "lights over the water," and his hand stops on the rope for one breath. Insight (15): it isn't surprise. It's the stillness of a man who has been told a thing like this before and hoped never to be again. He doesn't look at the west.

"There's a healer in the village," he says carefully. "Wren. She came up from the south as a girl and she's been here longer than I've had this rope. She wears an old wooden amulet, plain as a stick. If anyone knows about stones and lights, it's her. But she's not an easy woman. She'll test you before she talks. Don't flatter her."

The far bank rises out of the dusk, with plank walks over the shallows and low houses on stilts, new thatch bright against the old. You can smell smoke, eel and tar. The punt kisses the bank. Pim hands the rope to the dog, which lies down on it as though it were its job. He says, "The Long House is the big hall by the landing. She'll be there or near it." He looks at you a moment longer, then adds, "Mind the west. We don't go on the water out there after dark."

It's a quarter to six on a damp evening. You are at the edge of Reedholm with a lantern, your pole, a full set of slots, and 24 of 24 hit points. The few yellow lights ahead are lit.

What does Tamsin do?

DM: You press a third copper into Pim's palm and ask the dog's name. "Tar," he says, and the dog's tail thumps once at its name. You nod to both and walk up the plank walk. The planks are slick and sound, and the black water slides underfoot. You keep your eyes on the west the whole way. Out there nothing shows but mist and the last grey light, and a long low shape that might be an island and might be a trick of the dusk.

The Long House is the biggest hall on the landing, stilted and broad, with a lamp burning in every shutter. You knock plainly, three times.

The door opens before the third echo fades. The woman who stands there is in her late twenties, lean, in a plain apron over a worn wool dress, with dye-stained fingers and a face that gives nothing away. Around her neck, on a cord, hangs a small plain amulet of dark wood, rubbed smooth. She looks at the pole, at your boots, and at your hands. She does not look at your face for some time.

You say your name and what you've been dreaming. Her expression doesn't change. "Pim sent you," she says, flat. "He always does. He thinks I'm a priest." She turns without waiting. "Come in or don't, but shut the door. The damp gets in."

Inside, the hall is long and warm, and half its pallets are empty. A few are not. A woman nurses a baby by the stove, and an old man sleeps with his mouth open. At the far end a small girl of six or seven lies curled on a pallet, flushed and breathing fast. Wren walks straight to her. Without looking back, she hands you a lantern and a basin.

"I don't talk to people who've just walked in out of the rain with a dream," she says. "I talk to people who can hold a light steady. Sit there. Tell me what you see. Don't tell me what you think I want to hear."

The child on the pallet is pale and sweating, with a dry cough. Her lips are cracked, and one hand twitches against the blanket. Wren stands with her arms folded, watching you instead of the girl.

It's six o'clock on a damp evening. You have a lantern, a basin, a pole, and a full set of slots. What does Tamsin do?

Medicine (20): her breath is quick and shallow, about twice a sleeper's rate, with a wet rattle at the bottom of each one. Her lips are cracked and a dull blue-grey, and the cough is wet, not dry. Her skin is cool and clammy, not hot, and she shivers under a blanket that should be too heavy for her. Her fingertips are pale and faintly grey. Her pulse under your two fingers is fast and thready. You count against a slow sixty and get a hundred and forty. You turn her wrists to the light and find no red spots. You ask her name, and she whispers it to the ceiling. It's Nell.

You tell Wren what you see, and then the part you aren't sure of. "It's not the river-fever. No spots, no heat. It's a chill that's got into her chest, and the grey in her fingers is wrong. I've seen frostbite on a ferryman's hands after a winter crossing. She's not been out in the cold, has she?"

Wren is quiet for a long moment. Her face doesn't move, but the cord of the amulet draws tight against her neck. "No," she says. "She hasn't left the village in a month." She looks at the girl, not at you. "And the boiled water has been kept up, every pot, every morning. It isn't that fever. We beat that. It's something else."

She takes the basin from your hand. "Your eyes are honest," she says. "That's one." She sets the basin down and looks at you properly for the first time. "I can't cure her. I've tried everything I know. Willow-bark, honey, warm stones. It doesn't touch it. She's getting worse, and so are three others, and the grey in their hands is spreading. It started when the first of the Wick-fires came back."

It's ten past six on a damp evening in the Long House. You have a full set of slots, including Cure Wounds and Healing Word, and 24 of 24 hit points. Nell's breathing rattles under the lamp. Wren waits with her arms folded.

I didn't know they'd gone back to it until Nell took sick." She looks at you flatly. "All four. The same evening. I didn't want to say it to anyone. A grown woman telling a village the dead have been answering their children's candles."

DM: You eat the broth and keep your seat by the door, the pole across your knees, and you listen. The hall is full of ordinary night sounds: a baby's snuffle, the old man's breathing, the stove ticking as it cools, water lapping at the stilts. Wren sits two pallets down with her hands folded, awake, saying nothing.

Perception (20): for the first half hour there is nothing under the floor you can name. Then, at a quarter to eight, you hear it. It's very small, and it comes from the crawl between the stilts, beneath the corner where Fen, Ilsa, Bett and Nell lie. First there is a wet pat, then another, the sound of bare feet on damp mud. Then it stops, and under the boards, so low you would take it for the wind in the reeds, someone hums. It's a child's tune, a rhyme with no words. You know it, because Wren said it to you an hour ago. It starts and stops, and starts again.

You tell Wren with your eyes and a finger to your lips. She goes very still, and her hand closes on the amulet.

At eight, the fog you raised thins and lets go. You let the spell lapse rather than hold it with something beneath you. Through the shutter-crack you see the thin mist over the water, empty. Then she takes you by the sleeve, which she has not done before, and leads you along the front of the hall to the corner post by the landing stair. Carved into the grey wood in rows, in different hands and different years, are names. Under each is a small mark, a reed or a wave. They are the drowned. Most are old, worn smooth by hands. The last row is newer. Wren holds her lamp to it and puts her finger under the final name.

MINA TARR.

"Pim's," she says. "Not his blood. A fever-orphan he took in when he was sixteen and had nothing. She was six. Nine years ago, in the spring flood, the punt rolled at the Black Ford. He got out. She didn't. They never found her." She doesn't blink. "There's no grave. Pim won't say her name. He still tars that rope every month, and I think it's for her."

You take out the tallow stub you pocketed from the west walk, the one with the wick tied in a small neat knot. You show it to her. Wren looks at it a long time. Then her face does something it hasn't done all night. "She tied her wicks like that," she says. "She said it kept the light in. Half the village's children copied her, after. I thought it was just a game."

So the thing under the floor has a name, and the ford where it was lost is a mile back along the causeway. It is twenty-five past eight on a damp night. Stealth (14): the water is knee-deep and black, and you move through it in slow careful steps, with the rope paying out behind you and the cold gnawing at your boots. Nothing hears you. After fifteen minutes you stand at the mouth of a round chamber, about twenty-five feet across, with fitted stone walls and small niches cut in them. Old bones lie in each niche, laid out with care.

In the middle of the chamber, half in the water, lies a small bundle of bones in a rotted child's shift. A string of knotted wick is wound round the wrist. At the far end stands a sealed stone sarcophagus, rimed with white frost, and the cold comes from it like breath. Pale green sparks drift among the niches, slow as dust. A rotted wooden door stands in the west wall, shut. The sparks haven't noticed you.

It's twelve minutes to eight. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. The hooded lantern is at your belt, and the rope runs back to the stone. Tar waits on the shore. Pim is at the skiff, counting.

She pauses. "He died out on that fen, three weeks after he cured half this village. It was the same lights, and the same Isle." She doesn't look at you. "He's the reason I have my trade. I'd thank you not to make anything of that."

She is tiny, bright-eyed and bent like a hook, and she eats the honey with great ceremony before she speaks. Her tale, in her cracked voice, goes like this.

In the old days, she says, there were three keepers for the road the dead walk. One kept the lamp, one cut the reed, and one watched the wave. The Lamp-Keeper sat at the road's head, the Reed-Keeper at its heart, and the Wave-Keeper at its tail, where the road runs into water. "That's the Isle, girl. The seventh barrow, where the road meets the fen." The tomb under it, she says, belongs to the Wave-Keeper's wife, who was set to watch the water-road when the keepers made their seals. "They say she was buried standing, with her face to the west, and a stone on her breast so she'd never rise and walk." She wags a finger. "The frost isn't hers. The frost is what she's holding down."

She cannot say more. The tale breaks off in places, as old tales do. She doesn't know what the frost is, only that it came from the road, long ago. "The Lamp is the first seal," she says, "and the Wave is the last. If you break one, the others strain. A keeper told me that when I was a girl. I thought she meant a boat."

It's a quarter past eleven on Day 2. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. Gammer Hild watches you with her bright eyes, spoon in hand. Nothing stirs in the night. It is half past six on Day 3, and you have a rested morning. All your slots are full, and you still have 24 of 24 hit points. The night was too short to be a new long rest, so your prepared list stays as it is.

Now your questions. First, the spells. Neither Goodberry nor Entangle can secure the crack. The text of Goodberry only makes berries that heal one point each and feed a creature for a day. It can help the sick children eat, but it does nothing to a tomb. Entangle plants a 20-foot square of weeds, makes it difficult terrain, and restrains creatures that fail a Strength save, but it needs concentration and lasts a minute at most. It could hold a wisp-chaser or a drowner at the crack for a moment, as a tool in a fight. It couldn't seal anything for good. For that you need what you've named: salt, clay, fire, and living hands, and Ode's and Hild's counsel on how.

Second, the Wave-Keeper's marks. You try to recall what you saw on the fallen stone. Nature (7): you remember only that the wave was cut deeper than the lamp or the reed, and that the stone lay with its carved face up, as if someone had set it so on purpose when it fell. You can't read any more from memory. To learn more you would have to see the stone again, and you'd have to do it with the three lights on the other side of the mound.

Out past the shutters the day is grey and mild. Ode is up already, sitting on the landing with her arms on her knees, and Gammer Hild is being helped into the sun by a boy with a stool. Wren is at the stove with a pot of oats and a face that says she has already counted the children twice. The three pale lights are faint over the Isle in the dawn.

Then leave the light burning, and come back every dusk to light another. "The door doesn't care for iron," she says. "It cares for hands."

Wick and Gorm show you what they remember, standing on the landing with their arms folded and their faces grey. They sealed the crack once before, a quick rough job, with salt and a lump of clay and a prayer. It lasted a few years, then the tide scoured the clay out. This time, they say, it should be a thicker plug, set at low water, packed above the tideline and left to dry, and it should be done at first light while the lights are dozing. Gorm adds that he'd feel better with a ferrier at the pole, which is the nearest thing to a welcome he will give you.

By Wren's count and Ode's, the work is a day of gathering and mixing, clay carried from the bank, salt weighed, rushlights twisted, and the skiff made ready. You'll go out at first light on Day 4, which is tomorrow. It is a quarter past seven on Day 3, and you have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. The three lights are fading in the sun. The work is yours to start.

Nature (18): the plug comes out thick and tight and holds its shape when you press a thumb into it, and it doesn't crack when it dries in the sun. It must be rung over water by one who carries the living and the dead across, she says, with the lights' own door in front of them. "A ferrier," she says, and looks at your pole. "It's always a ferrier." She won't say more. She shuts her eyes and waves her spoon, and the old woman's face goes closed and small.

It's about one o'clock on Day 4. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. Reedholm's bell hangs on its post in the square, green with age, with no clapper. Pim will cross at dusk, and the west gap and the frost sarcophagus are as they were.

What does Tamsin do?

  [game] notebook: updated Hollow Isle
DM: You walk to the square and look the silent bell over. It hangs from an oak post that is older than any house nearby. It is bronze, as tall as your knee, and green all over with a crust of verdigris. The lip is thick and dull. The clapper is gone, and there is no ring or hook on the inside where one might have hung. Ode, watching from the Long House step, says the bell was never rung in her lifetime or her mother's, and that nobody remembers who hung it.

Investigation (11): you find the three marks cut round the shoulder, the lamp, the reed and the wave, much worn. You can see that a line of old script runs along the lip, but the verdigris has eaten it. You can't read a word. Sand and vinegar would clean it, and it would take an hour or so.

You ask Gammer Hild your questions, sitting beside her stool in the sun. Yes, she says, it must be rung from a boat, off the west gap, with the gap in front of you. As for a clapper, she waves one hand. "A bell with no tongue is struck with a hand and a word," she says. "A ferrier knows the word." You ask her what the word is. She frowns, sucks her gums, and shakes her head slowly. "If I knew it, girl, I'd have said it years ago." You can't tell whether that is the truth or only the truth as far as she remembers.

You ask Ode whether the bell can be taken down and carried. She considers it with her arms folded. "It can," she says. "It'll take four men and the winch from the eel-weir. I'll not have it done until you tell me it's needed. And I'll not have it done after dark." You say you won't do any of it until you understand the whole of the rite.

It is nearly two o'clock on Day 4. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. The bell hangs in the square in the sun, silent. Pim will cross at dusk to light the rushlight, and the west gap and the frost sarcophagus are as they were.

What does Tamsin do?

DM: You fetch sand and vinegar from Wren's stores and set to work on the lip of the bell, slowly, with a soft cloth, so that you don't scratch the old script. Tobb holds the bucket and doesn't talk, which is a mercy. The verdigris comes away in green flakes and then in a dull paste, and under it the bronze is the colour of dark honey.

Investigation (12), just enough. The letters are old river-Common, cut by a patient hand, and some are worn to ghosts. After an hour and some squinting you have it, running once round the lip:

CALL THE FARE. PAY THE FARE. HERE THE ROAD ENDS.

You sit back on your heels. You know those words, or the first of them. In the south, where you worked, the ferrier's cry at a landing is "Fare!" It is what you shout across the water to say you're ready to carry. Every ferrier calls it. And when a passenger dies on the crossing, or a body is brought to the water, a coin is laid on the tongue to pay the fare, so the ferrier will carry the dead across and not leave them standing on the bank. Your mother taught you that before she taught you to read a current. It is a kind of promise, not a charm.

Wren, called to look, stands at your shoulder with her arms folded and reads it twice. She says nothing for a long time. Then, flat: "Pay the fare. Brannoc never mentioned that." She frowns at the bell. "If it's a debt, you'd better know who owes it, and how much, before you ring."

You write the lines in your notes. Gammer Hild, shown the script, sits very still and then says, in a small voice, "Aye. That's the word." She doesn't say what it means to her. You can see she is troubled.

It's ten past three on Day 4. You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. The bell hangs clean and gleaming in the square. It is not yet dusk, and Pim waits at the landing for the hour to cross.

What does Tamsin do?

  [game] notebook: updated The Wave-Keeper's Bell
DM: "Then the fare is the question," you say. "Gammer, who owes it? The dead on the Isle, the Wave-Keeper's wife, or the village for not paying all these years?"

Insight (13), just enough. Hild answers slowly, and you believe her. The village owes it, she says. The first families who came to the fen swore to pay a fare for the held dead once a year, and they did, until the Burning. After that no one remembered the oath, and the village went on living on the debt. "The fare is not a coin, girl," she says. "A coin's the sign of it." Then she shuts her mouth and won't be moved.

You ask for your own side of it. In the southern trade, the family pays the fare for their dead, a coin laid on the tongue before the body goes into the boat. If there is no family, the ferrier pays out of their own purse, so the dead never stand on the bank unpaid. That has always been the law of the landing, and you have paid it twice yourself, for a stranger drowned off a barge and for a child no one claimed.

Wren fetches a copper from the Long House box and places it in your palm without a word. You tuck it into your pouch. When the afternoon is gone, you go to the landing with Pim and Tar and cross at dusk with a rushlight and a flint. You take the north shore, as before, with the mound between you and the lights.

You land at a quarter to seven. The plug is where you left it, dry and pale, with the first rushlight burnt to a black stub at its foot. The salt ring has not been scuffed. You strike a flame to the new rushlight and set it against the clay. Then, as you say the words again, "Hold, door; keep, light; the road ends here," something knocks from inside the plug.

It is three knocks, slow and soft, like knuckles on the far side of a door. I rolled for it, and that was the low result. Pim goes grey. Tar whines once and presses against your leg. The rushlight doesn't waver. In the west, the three pale lights lift and hum and drift in a slow uneasy circle round the gap, but they do not come. After a long minute the knocking stops, and the little flame stands straight.

You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. A copper is in your pouch. It is nearly seven on Day 4 and the light is going. The second rushlight is lit, and the lights are restless in the west.

What does Tamsin do?

== ROUND 180 ==
PLAYER: I keep my hand on Pim's shoulder and speak low and level toward the plug: "I hear you. I know the fare is owed, and I mean to pay it. Be patient a little longer."

Then I step back from the ring, nod to Pim, and we pole away quietly with the mound between us and the lights. Tomorrow I'll ask Gammer Hild and Wren who might be owed that copper, and what a whole village's debt would cost.

DM: You keep your hand on Pim's shoulder and speak low and level toward the plug: "I hear you. I know the fare is owed, and I mean to pay it. Be patient a little longer." The rushlight stands steady. Nothing answers, not a knock or a breath. After a moment you step back from the ring, nod to Pim, and the two of you pole away with the mound between you and the lights.

As for the knocking, you ask yourself whether it was one hand or many, and you think back. Perception (16): it was many. The knocks overlapped, with a layered rhythm, as of a crowd on the far side of a door, some quick and some slow and some barely there. But one hand was nearer and slower than the rest, three knocks to the breath, patient and clear. You can't say whether it was one voice that taught the others to wait. You have a cold feeling that it was.

Pim says nothing the whole way home. Tar lies across his boots with his chin on the gunwale, watching the dark water. The three lights sink into the dusk behind you and then are one smudge, then none.

You reach the Reedholm landing at about twenty-five to eight. A lantern burns in the Long House windows, and the village is shuttering itself for the night. You'll ask Gammer Hild and Wren tomorrow who might be owed that copper and what a whole village's debt would cost. For tonight there is supper and a pallet. Wren is waiting at the Long House door with her arms folded, counting you in.

You have 24 of 24 hit points, four 1st-level slots, two 2nd-level slots, and Wild Shape ready. The second rushlight is lit on the Isle, and the third will be needed tomorrow at dusk.
